            Elliptic Curves

                               J.S. Milne




Second Edition,
World Scientific Publishers.
     This book uses the beautiful theory of elliptic curves to introduce the reader to some
of the deeper aspects of number theory. It assumes only a knowledge of the basic algebra,
complex analysis, and topology usually taught in first-year graduate courses.
     An elliptic curve is a plane curve defined by a cubic polynomial. Although the problem
of finding the rational points on an elliptic curve has fascinated mathematicians since ancient
times, it was not until 1922 that Mordell proved that the points form a finitely generated
group. There is still no proven algorithm for finding the rank of the group, but in one of the
earliest applications of computers to mathematics, Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer discovered a
relation between the rank and the numbers of points on the curve computed modulo a prime.
Chapter IV of the book proves Mordell’s theorem and explains the conjecture of Birch and
Swinnerton-Dyer.
     Every elliptic curve over the rational numbers has an L-series attached to it. Hasse
conjectured that this L-series satisfies a functional equation, and in 1955 Taniyama suggested
that Hasse’s conjecture could be proved by showing that the L-series arises from a modular
form. This was shown to be correct by Wiles (and others) in the 1990s, and, as a consequence,
one obtains a proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem. Chapter IV of the book is devoted to
explaining this work.

Milne, James S. Elliptic curves. Second edition. World Scientific Publishing Co. Pte. Ltd.,
Hackensack, NJ, 2021. x+308 pp. ISBN: [9789811221859]; [9789811221835]

First edition published as: Milne, J. S. Elliptic Curves. Booksurge Publishers, Charleston,
SC, 2006.

Preface

In early 1996, I taught a course on elliptic curves. Since this was not long after Wiles had
proved Fermat’s Last Theorem and I promised to explain some of the ideas underlying his
proof, the course attracted an unusually large and diverse audience. As a result, I attempted
to make the course accessible to all students with a knowledge only of the standard first-year
graduate courses.
     When it was over, I collected the notes that I had handed out during the course into a
single file, made a few corrections, and posted them on the Web, where they were downloaded
tens of thousands of times.
     In 2006, I rewrote the notes and made them available as a paperback (the first edition of
this work). For this second edition, I have rewritten and updated the notes once again, while
retaining most of the numbering from the first edition.
     Beyond its intrinsic interest, the study of elliptic curves makes an excellent introduction
to some of the deeper aspects of current research in number theory. In the book, I have
attempted to place the theory of elliptic curves in this wider context, as well as in its historical
context.
     In reviewing the theory of elliptic curves, I have been struck by how much of it originated
with calculations: those of Ramanujan, which suggested Hecke operators and the Ramanujan
conjecture; those of Sato, which suggested the Sato–Tate conjecture in its general forms;
those of Selmer, which suggested the Cassels–Tate duality theorem; those of Birch and
Stephens, which suggested the Gross–Zagier formula; and, of course, those of Birch and
Swinnerton-Dyer, which suggested their conjecture and its generalizations.
     I wish to thank Rochelle Kronzek of World Scientific Publishers for encouraging me to
prepare this second edition.

Introduction

An elliptic curve over a field k is a nonsingular projective curve of genus 1 with a dis-
tinguished point. When the characteristic of k is not 2 or 3, it can be realized as a plane
projective curve

                     Y 2 Z D X 3 C aXZ 2 C bZ 3 ;        4a3 C 27b 2 ¤ 0:

The distinguished point is .0W 1W 0/. For example, the following pictures show the real points
(except the point at infinity) of two elliptic curves:




           Y 2 D X3 C 1                                              Y 2 D X.X 2    1/

     Although the problem of computing the points on an elliptic curve E with rational
numbers as coordinates has fascinated mathematicians since the time of the ancient Greeks,
it was not until 1922 that it was proved that it is possible to construct all the points starting
from a finite number by drawing chords and tangents. This is the famous theorem of Mordell,
which shows more precisely that the abelian group E.Q/ of rational points on E is finitely
generated. There is a simple algorithm for computing the torsion subgroup of E.Q/, but
there is still no proven algorithm for computing the rank. In one of the earliest applications
of computers to mathematics, Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer discovered experimentally a
relation between the rank and the numbers Np of the points on the curve read modulo the
different prime numbers p. The problem of proving this relation (the conjecture of Birch
and Swinnerton-Dyer) is one of the most important in mathematics. Chapter IV of the book
proves Mordell’s theorem and explains the conjecture of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer.
     In 1955, Taniyama noted that it was plausible that the Np attached to a given elliptic
curve always arise in a simple way from a modular form (in modern terminology, that the
elliptic curve is modular). In 1985 Frey observed that this did not appear to be true for the
elliptic curve attached to a nontrivial solution of the Fermat equation X n C Y n D Z n , n > 2.
His observation prompted Serre to revisit some old conjectures implying this, and Ribet
proved enough of his conjectures to deduce that Frey’s observation is correct: the elliptic
curve attached to a nontrivial solution of the Fermat equation is not modular. Finally, in
1994 Wiles (assisted by Taylor) proved that every elliptic curve in a large class is modular,

                                               1
2                                        Introduction


thereby proving Fermat’s Last Theorem. Chapter V of the book is devoted to explaining
these results.
    The first three chapters of the book develop the basic theory of elliptic curves.
    Elliptic curves have been used to shed light on some important problems that, at first
sight, appear to have nothing to do with them. I mention three such problems.

Fast factorization of integers
There is an algorithm for factoring integers that uses elliptic curves and is in many respects
better than previous algorithms. People have been factoring integers for millennia, but
recently the topic has become of practical significance: given an integer n that is the product
n D pq of two (large) primes p and q, there is a code for which everyone who knows n
can encode a message, but only those who know p and q can decode it. The security of the
code depends on no unauthorized person being able to factor n. See Koblitz 1994, VI, 4, or
Silverman and Tate 1992, IV, 4.

Lattices and the sphere packing problem
The sphere packing problem is that of finding an arrangement of n-dimensional unit balls
in Euclidean space that covers as much of the space as possible without overlaps. The
arrangement is called a lattice packing if the centres of the spheres are the points of a lattice
in n-space.
                                                              The best packing in
                                                              the plane is a lattice
                                                              packing.
Elliptic curves have been used to find lattice packings in many dimensions that are denser
than any previously known. See Chapter IV, 11.

Congruent numbers
A natural number n is said to be congruent if it is the area of a right-angle triangle whose
sides have rational length. For example, 6 is congruent because it is the area of a .3; 4; 5/
triangle. Such triangles occur in the writings of Diophantus, and the problem of determining
whether a given natural number n is congruent is stated already in an Arabic manuscript
from the tenth century (Dickson 1966). Fibonacci showed that 5 and 6 are congruent, Fermat
that 1; 2; 3; are not congruent, and Euler proved that 7 is congruent. However, an elementary
argument shows that n is congruent if and only if the elliptic curve
                                       Y 2 D X3     n2 X
has a point .x; y/ with x; y 2 Q and y ¤ 0, and it is only with recent advances in the study
of elliptic curves that significant progress has been made on the ancient problem. See p. 169.

Why are they called elliptic curves?
Computing the length of an arc of an ellipse leads to integrals that cannot be evaluated in
terms of elementary functions. These integrals were called “elliptic”, and the name was then
carried over to the curves on which they naturally lie (see p. 210). For a friendly explanation
of how elliptic integrals led to elliptic curves, and why ellipses are not elliptic curves (and
never will be), see Rice and Brown 2012.

P REREQUISITES
A knowledge of the basic algebra, analysis, and topology usually taught in advanced un-
dergraduate or first-year graduate courses. Some knowledge of algebraic geometry and
algebraic number theory will be useful but not essential.

N OTATION
We use the following standard notation: N is the set of natural numbers f0; 1; 2; : : :g, Z the
ring of integers, Q the field of rational numbers, R the field of real numbers, C the field of
complex numbers, and Fp the field with p elements. A number field is a finite extension of
Q.

commutative with 1, and homomorphisms of rings are required to map 1 to 1. For a ring A,

of a. The symbol log always means the natural logarithm.

Chapter I

Algebraic Curves

                                           Thus we see that many interesting curves can be
                                           defined as the zero sets of equations f .x; y/ D 0,
                                           where f .x; y/ is a polynomial in x and y. Such
                                           curves were called algebraic curves by Leibniz.
                                                          Brieskorn and Knörrer 1986, p. 78.

In this chapter, we develop some of the basic theory of algebraic curves, concentrating on
plane curves. In Section 3 we describe the group structure on a cubic curve.


1    Plane algebraic curves; Bezout’s theorem
We introduce plane algebraic curves, and develop the intersection theory of curves in the
plane.

Polynomial rings
We shall need to use that polynomial rings over fields are unique factorization domains.
This is proved by induction starting from the fact that a polynomial ring AŒX  is a unique
factorization domain if A is (CA, 4.9). As the units in kŒX1 ; : : : ; Xn  are just the nonzero
constants, every element f of the ring can be written as a product

                                      f D f1m1    frmr

of powers of irreducible polynomials fi with no fi a constant multiple of another, and the
factorization is unique up to replacing the fi with constant multiples. The repeated factors
of f are those fi with mi > 1. A polynomial f is irreducible if and only if the principal
ideal .f / is prime (CA, 4.1).

Affine plane curves
The affine plane over k is A2 .k/ D k  k. A nonconstant polynomial f 2 kŒX; Y , assumed
to have no repeated factor in k al ŒX; Y , defines an affine plane curve Cf over k whose points
with coordinates in any field K containing k are the zeros of f in K 2 ,

                           Cf .K/ D f.x; y/ 2 K 2 j f .x; y/ D 0g:

distinguished.

curve.

A SIDE 1.7 (a) In the definition of an affine plane curve, we require f to have no repeated factors
over k al so that our curves are geometrically reduced. Allowing repeated factors would allow the
curve to have multiple components, not just multiple points, and it would not be determined by its
points in k al .
    (b) (For the experts.) Essentially, we have defined an affine plane curve to be a geometrically
reduced closed subscheme of A2k of dimension 1. Such a scheme corresponds to an ideal in kŒX; Y 
of height one, which is principal because kŒX; Y  is a unique factorization domain. The polynomial
generating the ideal is uniquely determined by the scheme up to multiplication by a nonzero constant.

Intersection numbers
We wish to define the intersection number of curves Cf and Cg at a point P of Cf \ Cg .
For P D .0; 0/, the next proposition shows that there is exactly one reasonable way of doing
this.
    Let F.k/ be the set of pairs of polynomials f; g 2 kŒX; Y  having no common factor h
in kŒX; Y  with h.0; 0/ D 0.
    2 According to the usual definition, this is actually the negative of the discriminant.
                       1. Plane algebraic curves; Bezout’s theorem                           9


P ROPOSITION 1.8 There is a unique map I W F.k/ ! N such that
  (a) I.X; Y / D 1;
  (b) I.f; g/ D I.g; f / all .f; g/ 2 F.k/;
  (c) I.f; gh/ D I.f; g/ C I.f; h/ all .f; g/, .f; h/ 2 F.k/;
  (d) I.f; g C hf / D I.f; g/ all .f; g/ 2 F.k/, h 2 kŒX; Y ;
  (e) I.f; g/ D 0 if g.0; 0/ ¤ 0.
P ROOF. We first prove the uniqueness. Let f; g 2 F.k/. The theory of resultants (see 1.24

Projective plane curves
The projective plane over k is

                        P2 .k/ D f.x; y; z/ 2 k 3 j .x; y; z/ ¤ .0; 0; 0/g= ;

where .x; y; z/  .x 0 ; y 0 ; z 0 / if and only if there exists a c ¤ 0 in k such that .x 0 ; y 0 ; z 0 / D
.cx; cy; cz/. We write .xW yW z/ for the equivalence class of .x; y; z/ — the colon is meant to
suggest that only the ratios matter: Let P 2 P2 .k/; the triples .x; y; z/ representing P lie on
a single line L.P / through the origin in k 3 , and P 7! L.P / is a bijection from P2 .k/ to the
set of all such lines. Projective n-space Pn .k/, n 2 N, can be defined similarly.
    Let U2 .k/ D f.xW yW z/ j z ¤ 0g and L1 .k/ D f.xW yW z/ j z D 0g. Then

                                .x; y/ 7! .xW yW 1/ W A2 .k/ ! U2 .k/
                                .xW y/ 7! .xW yW 0/ W P1 .k/ ! L1 .k/

are bijections. Moreover, P2 .k/ is the disjoint union

                                       P2 .k/ D U2 .k/ t L1 .k/

of the “affine plane” U2 with the “line at infinity” L1 . The line

                          aX C bY C cZ D 0           (a and b not both zero)
12                               Chapter I. Algebraic Curves


meets L1 at the point . bW aW 0/, which depends only on the slope of the affine line aX C
bY C c D 0. We can think of P2 as being the affine plane U2 with exactly one point added
for each family of parallel lines.
    A nonconstant homogeneous polynomial F 2 kŒX; Y; Z, assumed to have no repeated
factor in k al , defines a projective plane curve CF over k whose points in any field K  k
are the zeros of F in P2 .K/,

                        CF .K/ D f.xW yW z/ 2 P2 .k/ j F .x; y; z/ D 0g:

Note that, because F is homogeneous,

                         F .cx; cy; cz/ D c deg F F .x; y; z/;   c 2 k;

and so, although it does not make sense to speak of the value of F at a point P of P2 , it does
make sense to say whether or not F is zero at P . Again, we do not distinguish the curves
CF and CcF with c 2 k  . A plane projective curve is (uniquely) a union of irreducible
projective plane curves (those defined by irreducible polynomials). The degree of F is called
the degree of the curve CF .

     The notions of tangent line, multiplicity, intersection number, etc. can be carried over
to projective curves by using that each point P of a projective curve C lies on at least one
of the affine curves Ci . If P 2 C.k/ is nonsingular as a point of one affine curve Ci , then
it is nonsingular as a point of all Ci on which it lies. Similar statements hold for the other
notions.

Bezout’s theorem
T HEOREM 1.18 (B EZOUT ) Let C and D be projective plane curves over k of degrees m
and n respectively having no common component. Then C and D intersect over k al in
exactly mn points counted with multiplicity, i.e.,
                                X
                                          I.P; C \ D/ D mn:
                               P 2C.k al /\D.k al /

P ROOF. For an elementary proof, see Fulton 1969, 5.3.                                                2

    Let C and D be projective plane curves over k of degrees m and n respectively. If there
are mn points in C.k al / \ D.k al /, then Bezout’s theorem and 1.13 show that the points must
be nonsingular on both C and D; if there are more than mn points, then the curves must
have a common component.
A SIDE 1.19 Over C, the map
                                                X
                               F; G 7!                      I.P; CF \ CG /
                                         P 2CF .C/\CG .C/

is continuous in the coefficients of F and G for the discrete topology on N. Thus, the total intersection
number is constant on continuous families of homogeneous polynomials, which allows us, in proving
Bezout’s theorem, to take F and G to be X m and Y n . Then P D .0W 0W 0/ is the only point on both
curves, and
                                I.P; CX m \ CY n / D I.X m ; Y n / D mn,
which proves the theorem. A similar argument works over any field once one has shown that the total
intersection number is constant on algebraic families (cf. Shafarevich 1994, III, 2.2).

E XAMPLE 1.20 According to Bezout’s theorem, a curve of degree m will meet the line at
infinity in exactly m points counted with multiplicity. Our favourite curve

                                CW      Y 2 Z D X 3 C aXZ 2 C bZ 3
14                                     Chapter I. Algebraic Curves


meets L1 at a single point P D .0W 1W 0/, but (see 1.8)

                            I.P; L1 \ C / D I.Z; Z            X3     aXZ     bZ 3 /
                                               D I.Z; X 3 /
                                               D 3:

In general, a nonsingular point P on a curve C is called a point of inflection (or flex) if the
intersection multiplicity of the tangent line and C at P is  3.3 If the intersection multiplicity
equals 3, then the point of inflection is said to be ordinary. This is always the case for cubic
curves.




                                          Z D X 3 C XZ 2 C Z 3


R EMARK 1.21 According to Bezout’s theorem, any two irreducible components of a pro-
jective plane curve have a common point (possibly in a finite extension of k), which will
be singular by 1.4b. Therefore, nonsingular projective plane curves are geometrically
irreducible.

A SIDE 1.27 There is a geometric interpretation of the last proposition. Take k to be algebraically
closed, and regard the coefficients of F and G as indeterminates. Let V be the subset of AmCnC2 
P1 where both F .s0 ; : : : ; sm I X; Y / and G.t0 ; : : : ; tn I X; Y / vanish. The proposition says that the
projection of V on AmCnC2 is the set where Res.F; G/, regarded as a polynomial in the si and ti ,
vanishes. In other words, the proposition tells us that the projection of the Zariski-closed set V is the
Zariski-closed set defined by the resultant of F and G.
     Elimination theory does this more generally. Given polynomials Pi .T1 ; : : : ; Tm I X0 ; : : : ; Xn /,
homogeneous in the Xi , it provides an algorithm for finding polynomials Rj .T1 ; : : : ; Tm / such that
the Pi .a1 ; : : : ; am I X0 ; : : : ; Xn / have a common zero if and only if Rj .a1 ; : : : ; am / D 0 for all j . See,
for example, Cox et al. 2007, Chap. 8, 5.

N OTES The history of plane algebraic curves goes back more than 2000 years — see the first chapter
of Brieskorn and Knörrer 1986. Newton made the first systematic study of real cubic plane curves,
classifying them into 72 cases (but missing 6).


2     Rational points on plane curves
For a projective plane curve CF over Q, the two fundamental questions are the following:
  (a) Does CF have a point with coordinates in Q, i.e., does F .X; Y; Z/ have a nontrivial
      zero in Q?
  (b) If the answer to (a) is yes, can we describe the set of points?
One can also ask whether there are algorithms to answer these questions. For example, we
may know that a curve has only finitely many points without having an algorithm to find the
points or even determine their number.
    In this section, we give an overview of the topic. For simplicity, we assume throughout
that C D CF is geometrically irreducible, i.e., that F .X; Y; Z/ is irreducible in QŒX; Y; Z
and remains so over Qal .
    We first make an observation that will be useful throughout the book. Let

                                                               aij X i Y j
                                                           P
                                            f .X; Y / D

be a polynomial with coefficients aij in a field k, and let K be a Galois extension of k
(possibly infinite). If .a; b/ 2 K  K is a zero of f .X; Y /, then so also is . a;  b/ for all
 2 Gal.K=k/ because

                                      aij . a/i . b/j D  .        aij ai b j / D f .a; b/ D 0.
                                  P                              P
                f .a; b/ D

Thus, Gal.K=k/ acts on Cf .K/. More generally, if C1 ; C2 ; : : : are affine plane curves over
k, then Gal.K=k/ stabilizes the subset C1 .K/ \ C2 .K/ \ : : : of K  K. On applying this
remark to the curves
                                        @f          @f
                             f D 0;          D 0;       D 0;
                                        @X          @Y
we see that Gal.K=Q/ stabilizes the set of singular points of Cf . Similar remarks apply to
projective plane curves.

Curves of degree one
In this case, the curve is a line

                          C W aX C bY C cZ D 0;               a; b; c 2 Q, not all zero:
18                              Chapter I. Algebraic Curves


It always has points with coordinates in Q, and it is possible to parameterize the points —
for example, if c ¤ 0, then the map
                                                      a      b
                                  .sW t / 7! .sW tW   cs     c t/

is a bijection from P1 .K/ onto C.K/ for all fields K  Q.

Curves of degree two
In this case F .X; Y; Z/ is a quadratic form in 3 variables, and C is a conic. Note that C
cannot be singular, because a nontangent line L through a point P of multiplicity m  2 will
meet C in a second point Q and the total intersection number
                                                             1:13
                           I.P; L \ C / C I.Q; L \ C /  m C 1

violates Bezout’s theorem.
    Sometimes it is easy to see that C.Q/ is empty. For example,

                                       X 2 C Y 2 C Z2

has no nontrivial zero in Q because it has no nontrivial zero in R. Similarly,

                                       X2 C Y 2       3Z 2

has no nontrivial zero because, if it did, then it would have a zero .x; y; z/ with x; y; z 2 Z
and gcd.x; y; z/ D 1; the only squares in Z=3Z are 0 and 1, and so

                      x2 C y2  0     mod 3 H) x  0  y             mod 3I

but then 3 divides z, which contradicts our assumption that gcd.x; y; z/ D 1.
    In 1785, Legendre showed that such arguments can be made into an effective procedure
for deciding whether CF .Q/ is nonempty. He first shows that, by an elementary change of
variables, F can be put in diagonal form

                F D aX 2 C bY 2 C cZ 2 ;      a; b; c 2 Z;      a; b; c square free.

He then shows that if abc ¤ 0 and a; b; c are not all of the same sign, then F .X; Y; Z/ has a
nontrivial zero in Q if and only if bc, ca, ab are squares modulo a; b; c respectively.
It follows from the quadratic reciprocity law (IV, 8.5), that this implies that there exists an
integer m, depending in a simple way on the coefficients of F , such that CF .Q/ ¤ ; if and
only if F .X; Y; Z/  0 mod m has a solution in integers relatively prime to m. See 2.18.
     Now suppose that C has a point P0 with coordinates in Q. Can we describe all the
points? A line over Q always meets C in exactly two points counting multiplicities (Bezout’s
theorem). If the points coincide, then the line is tangent to C . Otherwise, the pair of points
is stable under the action of Gal.Qal =Q/, and so, if one has coefficients in Q, then both do.
The lines through P0 in P2 form a “P1 ”, and the map sending a line through P0 to its second
point of intersection is a bijection from P1 .Q/ to C.Q/. For example, take P0 to be the
point . 1W 0W 1/ on the curve C W X 2 C Y 2 D Z 2 . The line bX aY C bZ, a; b 2 Q, of slope
b=a through P0 meets C at the point .a2 b 2 W 2ab W a2 C b 2 /. In this way, we obtain a
parameterization .aW b/ 7! .a2 b 2 W 2abW a2 C b 2 / of the points of C with coordinates in Q.
                                   2. Rational points on plane curves                                     19


Curves of degree 3
Let C W F .X; Y; Z/ D 0 be a projective plane curve over Q of degree 3. If C is singular, then
Bezout’s theorem shows that it has only one singular point and that its multiplicity is 2. The
singular point P0 will have coordinates in some finite extension K of Q, which we may take
to be Galois over Q, but Gal.K=Q/ stabilizes the set of singular points in C.K/, hence fixes
P0 , and so P0 2 C.Q/. Now a line through P0 will meet the curve in exactly one other point
(possibly coincident with P0 ), and so we again get a parameterization of the points of C
with coordinates in Q.
     Nonsingular cubics will be the subject of the rest of the book. We shall see that
Legendre’s arguments fail for nonsingular cubic curves. For example,

                                             3X 3 C 4Y 3 C 5Z 3

has nontrivial real zeros and nontrivial zeros modulo m for all integers m, but it has no
nontrivial zero in Q.
    Let C be a nonsingular cubic curve over Q. According to Bezout’s theorem, a line meets
the curve in three points counting multiplicities. If two of the points have coefficients in Q,
so also does the third.4 Thus, the chord through two points in C.Q/ meets the curve in a
third point in C.Q/, and the tangent line at a point in C.Q/ meets the curve at another point
in C.Q/.
    In a famous paper, published in 1922, Mordell proved the following theorem.

T HEOREM 2.1 (F INITE BASIS THEOREM ) Let C be a nonsingular cubic curve over Q.
There exists a finite set of points in C.Q/ from which every other point in C.Q/ can be
obtained by successive chord and tangent constructions.

In fact, C.Q/, if nonempty, has a natural structure of a commutative group (see the next
section), and the finite basis theorem says that C.Q/ is finitely generated. There is as yet no
proven algorithm for finding the rank of the group.

R EMARK 2.2 For a singular cubic curve C over Q, the nonsingular points still form a
group, which may be isomorphic to .Q; C/ or .Q ; / (see II, 3), neither of which is
finitely generated — the elements of any finitely generated subgroup of .Q; C/ have a
common denominator, and a finitely generated subgroup of .Q ; / can contain only finitely
many prime numbers. Thus, the finite basis theorem fails for singular cubics.

Curves of genus > 1
The genus of a nonsingular projective curve C over Q is the genus of the Riemann surface
C.C/. More generally, the genus of an arbitrary curve over Q is the genus of the nonsingular
projective curve attached to its function field (Fulton 1969, 7.5, Cor. to Theorem 3). Mordell
conjectured in his 1922 paper, and Faltings (1983) proved, that every curve of genus > 1 has
only finitely many points with coordinates in Q. This applies to every nonsingular projective
plane curve of degree at least 4 and to every singular projective plane curve whose degree
is sufficiently large compared to the multiplicities of its singularities (see 6.5 later in this
chapter).
    4 On substituting Y D aX C b into the polynomial defining the affine cubic, one obtains a cubic polynomial

in X with coefficients in Q, two of whose roots lie in Q; it follows that the third root also lies Q.
20                                  Chapter I. Algebraic Curves


R EMARK 2.3 Let P 2 P2 .Q/. Choose a representative .aW bW c/ for P with a; b; c integers
having no common factor, and define the height H.P / of P to be max.jaj; jbj; jcj/. For any
B > 0, there are only finitely many P with H.P / < B.
     For a curve C of genus > 1, there is an effective bound for the number of points
P 2 C.Q/ but no known effective bound, in terms of the polynomial defining C , for the
heights of the points P 2 C.Q/. With such a bound, the points in C.Q/ could be found by a
finite search. See Hindry and Silverman 2000, F.4.2, for a discussion of this problem.
A SIDE 2.4 There is a heuristic explanation for Mordell’s conjecture. Let C be a curve of genus
g  1 over Q. If C.Q/ is nonempty, then C embeds into its jacobian variety J , which is a projective
variety of dimension g with a group structure, i.e., an abelian variety. A generalization of the finite
basis theorem, due to Weil, says that J.Q/ is finitely generated. Hence, inside the g-dimensional set
J.C/ we have the countable set J.Q/ and the (apparently unrelated) one-dimensional set C.C/. If
g > 1, it would be an extraordinary coincidence if the second set contained more than a finite number
of elements from the first set.

     To continue our discussion, we shall need the field of p-adic numbers.

A brief introduction to the p-adic numbers
Let p be a prime number. A nonzero rational number a can be uniquely expressed as
a D pr m                                                                                  1
        n , where m; n; r are integers and m and n are not divisible by p. We set jajp D p r ,
and we let j0jp D 0. We call jajp the p-adic valuation of a. It has the following properties:
  (a) jajp D 0 if and only if a D 0,
  (b) jabjp D jajp jbjp ,
  (c) ja C bjp  maxfjajp ; jbjp g      . jajp C jbp j/.
These statements imply that
                                                  def
                                        dp .a; b/ D ja    bjp
is a translation-invariant metric on Q, called the p-adic metric. Note that for rational
numbers a; b to be close for this metric means that their difference is divisible by a high
power of p. The field Qp of p-adic numbers is the completion of Q for this metric. We now
explain what this means.
    A sequence a1 ; a2 ; a3 ; : : : of rational numbers is a Cauchy sequence for the p-adic
metric if, for each real number " > 0, there exists a natural number N."/ such that

                          jam     an jp < "    whenever m; n > N."/:

A sequence a1 ; a2 ; a3 ; : : : is said to converge to a 2 Q if; for each real number " > 0, there
exists a natural number N."/ such that

                              jan    ajp < "     whenever n > N."/:

Let R be the set of all Cauchy sequences in Q for the p-adic metric. It becomes a ring with
the obvious operations. An element of R is said to be null if it converges to zero. The set of
null sequences is an ideal I in R, and Qp is defined to be the quotient ring R=I . It is a field.
     If ˛ D .an /n1 is a Cauchy sequence in Q, then property (c) implies that the sequence
jan jp becomes constant for large n, and we set this constant value equal to j˛jp . The
map ˛ 7! j˛jp W R ! Q factors through Qp and has the properties (a), (b), (c) listed above.
Therefore the p-adic metric on Q extends to Qp .

Curves of degree 2 and 3 continued
Clearly, a necessary condition for a curve to have a point with coordinates in Q is that it
have points with coordinates in R and all the fields Qp . Indeed, we observed above that
X 2 C Y 2 C Z 2 has no nontrivial zero in Q because it has no nontrivial real zero, and our
argument for X 2 C Y 2 3Z 2 shows that it has no nontrivial zero in the field Q3 of 3-adic
numbers. A modern interpretation of Legendre’s theorem is that, for curves of degree 2, the
condition is also sufficient.

T HEOREM 2.8 (L EGENDRE ) A quadratic form F .X; Y; Z/ with coefficients in Q has a
nontrivial zero in Q if and only if it has nontrivial zeros in R and in Qp for all p.
P ROOF. See Cassels 1991, Chapter 3, or Serre 1973, Chapter IV.                                       2

      See 2.18 below for the relation of this statement to Legendre’s original statement.
A SIDE 2.9 Theorem 2.8 is true for quadratic forms in any number of variables (Hasse–Minkowski
theorem). See Serre 1973, Chap. IV, for a good exposition of the proof. The key cases are 3 and 4
variables .2 is easy, and for  5 variables, one uses induction on n), and the key result needed for its
proof is the quadratic reciprocity law (IV, 8.5).

    If for some class of polynomials (better algebraic varieties) it is known that a polynomial
(or variety) has a zero in Q if and only if it has zeros in R and all Qp , then one says that the
Hasse, or local-global, principle holds for the class.

    Recall that Legendre showed that f D 0 has a nonzero solution if and only if

  (b)   bc, ca, ab are squares modulo a, b; c respectively.
     We prove the necessity of the conditions (a) and (b). Suppose that f D 0 has a nonzero solution
.x; y; z/ 2 Q3 . Then certainly (a) holds. We may suppose that x; y; z 2 Z and are relatively prime in
pairs. If x and c had a prime factor p in common, then p would divide y, contradicting our assumption.
Thus, x is invertible modulo c, and from ax 2 C by 2 C cz 2 D 0 we deduce that ab D .byx 1 /2
modulo c. Similarly, bc and ca are squares modulo a and b.

                          3. The group structure on a plane cubic curve                                  25


N OTES The tangent process for constructing new rational points goes back to Diophantus (c250
A.D.) and was “much loved by Fermat”; the chord process was known to Newton. Hilbert and
Hurwitz showed (in 1890) that, if a curve of genus zero has one rational point, then it has infinitely
many, all given by rational values of a parameter. In 1901, Poincaré published a long article on
rational points on curves in which he attempted to rescue the subject from being merely a collection
of ad hoc results about individual equations. Although he is usually credited with conjecturing the
finite basis theorem, he rather simply assumed it. Beppo Levi was the first to ask explicitly whether
the finite basis theorem was true. In his remarkable 1922 paper, Mordell proved the finite basis
theorem, and, in a rather off-handed way, conjectured that all curves of genus > 1 over Q have only
finitely many rational points (the Mordell conjecture). Both were tremendously important, the former
as the first general theorem in diophantine geometry, and the latter as one of the most important open
conjectures in the subject until it was proved by Faltings in 1983.
     For more on the history of these topics, see Cassels 1986, Schappacher 1990, and Schappacher
and Schoof 1996, from which the above notes have largely been drawn.


3    The group structure on a plane cubic curve
Let C be a nonsingular projective plane curve of degree 3 over a field k, which, for simplicity,
we take to be perfect. Assume that C.k/ is nonempty, and choose a point O 2 C.k/. In this
section, we give a geometric construction of a group structure on C.k/ having O as its zero
element.
     According to Bezout’s theorem, a line meets the curve in three points counting multiplic-
ities. Given P; Q 2 C.k/, neither lying on the tangent line at the other, we let PQ denote
the third point of intersection of the line through P and Q with C . Given P 2 C.k/, we let
PP denote the second point of intersection of the tangent line at P with C . The points PQ
and PP have coordinates in k.5 Note that PP D P if and only if P is a point of inflection.
     For a pair P; Q 2 C.k/, we define

                                          P C Q D O.PQ/;

i.e., we draw the line through P and Q to get PQ, and then the line through O and PQ to
get P C Q, as in the following diagram:

Geometric proof of associativity
In proving associativity, we may replace k with a larger field, and so we may assume k to be
algebraically closed.

cubic curve passing through eight of the points also passes through the ninth.

natural to take it to be the zero for the group law. The lines through it are the vertical lines in
the affine plane, and so .xW yW 1/C .x 0 W y 0 W 1/ D O if and only if x D x 0 and y C y 0 D 0. In
particular, the points of order 2 on E are the points .xW 0W 1/ with x a root of x 3 C ax C b.
N OTES In his papers in the 1920s in which he generalized Mordell’s finite basis theorem, Weil made
systematic use of the commutative group structure on E.Q/, and (according to Schappacher 1990)
was perhaps the first to do so — earlier mathematicians worked instead with the binary operation
P; Q 7! PQ D .P C Q/, which is not associative.

R EMARK 4.7 Riemann’s inequality was originally proved for meromorphic functions on
compact Riemann surfaces. Since the rational functions on a nonsingular projective curve
over C coincide the meromorphic functions on its associated Riemann surface, the two have
the same genus.

curve. For a nonsingular projective plane curve, it is given by the formula,

                                         .deg C    1/.deg C     2/

(Fulton 1969, 8.3, Proposition 3). For example, a nonsingular projective plane curve has
genus 0 if it is of degree 1 or 2 and it has genus 1 if it is of degree 3.

defined (see 6), there do exist nonsingular projective curves of arbitrary genus g — just not
nonsingular projective plane curves.

T HE R IEMANN –ROCH THEOREM
Let C be a nonsingular projective plane curve over k. For a divisor D on C , we define
L.D/ D f' j div.'/ C D  0g [ f0g, as before, and we let `.D/ denote its dimension as a k-
vector space. There is a “canonical” divisor KC on C , well defined up to linear equivalence,
which equals the divisor of any differential one-form on C . The Riemann–Roch theorem
states the following.

T HEOREM 4.13 (R IEMANN –ROCH ) For any divisor D on C ,

                           `.D/ D deg.D/ C 1       g C `.KC      D/

where KC is a canonical divisor; moreover, KC has degree 2g 2, and so `.D/ D deg.D/ C
1 g if deg.D/ > 2g 2.

    The usual proofs of the Riemann–Roch theorem apply over arbitrary fields. As an
alternative, we sketch a deduction of Theorem 4.13 from the same theorem over k al . Fix an
algebraic closure k al and let CN denote C regarded as a curve over k al . The Galois group
   D Gal.k al =k/ acts on C.k al / D CN .k al / with finite orbits because each P 2 C.k al / has
coordinates in some finite extension of k.
    Let Ci be one of the standard affine pieces of C , and let p be a prime divisor on Ci . To
give a k-homomorphism kŒCi  D kŒx; y ! k al amounts to giving an element of Ci .k al /,
and the homomorphisms whose kernel contains p correspond to the points in a single -orbit
in C.k al /. In this way, we obtain a bijection from the set of prime divisors on C to the set of
  -orbits in C.k al /. Thus, we have a injective homomorphism

                                      N Div.C / ! Div.CN /
                                 D 7! DW
                                     P
whose image consists of the divisors nP P such that nP is constant on each -orbit. The
map preserves principal divisors, the degrees of divisors, and the dimensions ` (because the
condition for a function to lie in L.D/ is linear). It follows that C and CN have the same
genus, and that the Riemann–Roch theorem for CN implies it for C .

Regular maps of curves
Throughout this section, k is a perfect field. All curves are required to be geometrically
irreducible. By an open subset of a curve, we mean the complement of a finite set of points.

A FFINE PLANE CURVES
A regular map 'W Cg1 ! Cg2 of affine plane curves is a pair .f1 ; f2 / of regular functions
on Cg1 sending Cg1 .K/ into Cg2 .K/ for all fields K containing k, i.e., such that, for all

map determines the pair .f1 ; f2 /.

P ROJECTIVE PLANE CURVES
Consider polynomials F0 .X; Y; Z/; F1 .X; Y; Z/, F2 .X; Y; Z/ of the same degree. The map

               .a0 W a1 W a2 / 7! .F0 .a0 ; a1 ; a2 /W F1 .a0 ; a1 ; a2 /W F2 .a0 ; a1 ; a2 //

defines a regular map to P2 on the subset of P2 where not all Fi vanish. Its restriction to any
curve in P2 will also be regular where it is defined. It may be possible to extend the map to a
larger set by representing it by different polynomials. Conversely, every regular map to P2
from an open subset of P2 arises in this way, at least “locally”. Rather than give a precise

5     Defining algebraic curves over subfields
Let ˝  k be fields. A curve C over k is defined by polynomial equations with coefficients
in k, and these same equations define a curve C˝ over ˝. In this section, we examine the
functor C       C˝ . This functor is faithful (the map on Hom sets is injective) but, in general,
it is neither full (the map on Homs is not onto) nor essentially surjective (a curve over ˝
need not be isomorphic to a curve of the form C˝ ).

R EMARK 5.4 Let C be a nonsingular projective curve over an algebraically closed field.
     If C has genus zero, then it is isomorphic to P1 . The automorphisms of P1 are the linear
fractional transformations, and so any three distinct points rigidify P1 .
     If C has genus one, then it has only finitely many automorphisms fixing a given point
O on C (see II, 2; IV, 7.13, 7.14). For each automorphism ˛ ¤ id, choose a P such that
˛.P / ¤ P . Then O and the points P rigidify C .
     If C has genus greater than one, then it has only finitely many automorphisms (see, for
example, Hartshorne 1977, IV, Exercise 5.2, when the field has characteristic zero). Thus, C
is rigidified by some finite set.

is continuous if and only if the stabilizer of each P 2 C.˝/ is open.

P ROPOSITION 5.5 Assume ˝ is algebraically closed. The functor sending a nonsingular
projective curve C over k to C˝ endowed with the natural action of on C.˝/ is fully
faithful, with essential image the nonsingular projective curves endowed with a continuous
regular action of .

P ROOF. Because ˝ is algebraically closed, to give a descent system on a curve C 0 over ˝ is
the same as giving a regular action of on C 0 .˝/. Moreover, a regular map of curves with
descent systems will preserve the descent systems if and only if it commutes with the actions.
According to 5.4, C 0 is rigidified by a finite set S , and because the action is continuous the
condition in 5.3 holds. Thus the statement follows from the preceding propositions.            2



6    General algebraic curves
We saw in Remark 4.8 that not all algebraic curves can be realized as nonsingular plane
curves. In this section, we define more general algebraic curves. These will be needed
mainly in Chapter V. Throughout, the field k is perfect.

Affine algebraic curves
An ideal a in a ring R is radical if R=a has no nilpotent elements, and it is prime if R=a is
an integral domain. For example, the ideal .f / in kŒX; Y  is radical if and only if f has no
multiple factors, and it is prime if and only if f is irreducible. An ideal a D .f1 ; : : : ; fr / in
kŒX1 ; : : : ; Xn  defines an ideal in k al ŒX1 ; : : : ; Xn  with the same generators.
    The affine n-space over k is An .k/ D k n . An ideal a in kŒX1 ; : : : ; Xn , assumed to be
radical and remain radical in k al ŒX1 ; : : : ; Xn ; defines an affine algebraic variety Va over
k whose points in any field K  k are its zeros, i.e., Va .K/ is the set of .x1 ; : : : ; xn / 2 K n
such that f .x1 ; : : : ; xn / D 0 for all f 2 a (equivalently, for all f in a set of generators for a).
    The variety Va is irreducible if a is prime, and geometrically irreducible if a remains
prime in k al ŒX1 ; : : : ; Xn . A radical ideal a can be written as a finite intersection a D
p1 \    \ pr of prime ideals, and then

                           Va .K/ D Vp1 .K/ [    [ Vpr .K/;     K  k;

with each Vpi irreducible. The varieties Vpi are called the (irreducible) components of Va .
    The dimension of an irreducible variety Vp is zero if Vp .k al / consists of a single point. It is
one, and the variety is called a curve, if its maximal proper closed subvarieties have dimension
zero. Inductively, the dimension of Vp is m if its maximal proper closed subvarieties have
dimension m 1.
    Let P D .a1 ; : : : ; an / 2 Vp .K/. The tangent space TP .Va / to Va at P is the linear
subspace (centred at P ) defined by the equations
                                    X  @f 
                                                  .Xi ai / D 0

where f runs over the elements of a (equivalently, over a set of generators for a). Then
dimK .TP .Va //  dim V , with equality if P nonsingular.
42                               Chapter I. Algebraic Curves


Projective algebraic curves
An ideal a in kŒX0 ; : : : ; Xn  is homogeneous if, with each f , it contains the homogeneous
components of f . A projective algebraic variety in Pn is defined by a homogeneous ideal
a in kŒX0 ; : : : ; Xn . Each projective algebraic variety in Pn is, in a natural way, a union of
n C 1 affine algebraic varieties. The definitions and statements in Section 4 carry over in an
obvious way to projective algebraic curves. For example, the Riemann–Roch theorem holds
for all nonsingular projective algebraic curves, and Propositions 4.18, 4.23, 4.24 hold with
“plane” omitted. It is not necessary to assume that the curves in 5 are plane.

Nonsingular projective curves, plane curves, and function fields
P ROPOSITION 6.1 Let Cf be an irreducible affine plane curve over k. The field k.Cf / is
finitely generated of transcendence degree 1 over k, and every such field arises from a curve.
The curve Cf is geometrically irreducible if and only if k is algebraically closed in k(Cf /.
P ROOF. By definition, k.Cf / is the field of fractions of kŒX:Y =.f /, and so it is obviously
finitely generated of transcendence degree 1. Conversely, let F be finitely generated of
transcendence degree 1 over k. By definition, this means that F is a finite extension of
k.X / for some transcendental X in F . Using that k is perfect, one shows that X can be
chosen so that F is a separable extension of k.X /, and so F D k.X /.y/ for some y. Let
f .X; Y / 2 k.X/ŒY  be the minimum polynomial of y over k.X /. After multiplying f by a
power of X, we may suppose that it lies in kŒX:Y . Now F D k.Cf /.
     Let k 0 be the algebraic closure of k in k.Cf /. Then

                      k al ˝k k 0  k al ˝k kŒX; Y =.f / ' k al ŒX; Y Œ=.f /;

and so k al ŒX; Y =.f / is not an integral domain if k 0 ¤ k. We omit the proof of the converse.2
D EFINITION 6.2 A function field (in one variable) over k, is a field F containing k and
such that
  (a) k is algebraically closed in F ;
   (b) F is finitely generated (of transendence degree 1) over k.
     Thus, the functor C     k.C / from irreducible affine algebraic curves over k to function
fields in one variable over k is essentially surjective.
T HEOREM 6.3 The functor C       k.C / is a contravariant equivalence from the category of
geometrically irreducible nonsingular projective curves over k to the category of function
fields in one variable over k.
P ROOF. In particular, when we allow nonsingular projective curves other than plane curves,
then the functor C      k.C / becomes essentially surjective (cf. 4.18). Let F be a function
field in one variable over k. From Proposition 6.1, we know that F D k.C / for C an affine
curve over k. The next theorem allows us to replace C with a nonsingular projective curve.2
T HEOREM 6.4 Let C be a projective plane curve over a perfect field k. There exists a
nonsingular projective curve C 0 over k and a surjective morphism 'W C 0 ! C such that
  (a) the homomorphism k.C / ! k.C 0 / defined by ' is an isomorphism;
  (b) '.k al /W C 0 .k al / ! C.k al / restricts to a bijection U 0 ! U , where U is the set of
      nonsingular points in C.k al / and U 0 is its inverse image in C 0 .k al /.
                                      6. General algebraic curves                                       43


If ' 0 W C 00 ! C is a second such curve and morphism, then there is a unique isomorphism
  W C 0 ! C 00 such that ' 0 ı D '. Every nonsingular projective curve over k arises in this
way.

P ROOF. See Lorenzini 1996 or Fulton 1969, Chap. 7.                                                       2

A SIDE 6.5 For any nonsingular projective curve C , there exists a regular map 'W C ! C 0 from C
onto a plane projective curve C 0 such that ' is an isomorphism outside a finite set and C 0 has only
ordinary multiple points as singularities (Fulton 1969, Chap. 7). The genus of C is given by

                           .deg C 0    1/.deg C 0   2/     X mP .C 0 /.mP .C 0 /      1/
                 g.C / D                                                                   ;
                                        2                               2
                                                         P 2C.k/

where mP .C 0 / is the multiplicity of P on C 0 (ibid., Chap. 8, 3).

A SIDE 6.6 Theorem 6.3 shows that the study of nonsingular projective curves is essentially equiv-
alent to that of function fields in one variable. This observation, which goes back to Dedekind
and Weber 1882 in the complex case,6 allows one to study algebraic curves by the methods of
algebraic number theory, and makes the subject quite elementary. It was the approach used by the
German school from Dedekind and Weber in the 1880s to Deuring and Hasse in the 1950s. Although
elementary, this approach misses the geometry, and works well only in dimension one.




    6 They used algebraic methods to give rigorous proofa of statements about Riemann surfaces that had only

previously been proved, in a nonrigorous way, using analysis and topology.

1     Definition of an elliptic curve
D EFINITION 1.1 An elliptic curve over k can be defined, according to taste, as
  (a) a nonsingular projective plane curve E over k of degree 3 together with a point
      O 2 E.k/;
    (b) same as (a) except that O is required to be a point of inflection;
    (c) a nonsingular projective plane curve over k of the form

                  Y 2 Z C a1 X Y Z C a3 Y Z 2 D X 3 C a2 X 2 Z C a4 XZ 2 C a6 Z 3 I       (9)

    (d) a nonsingular complete curve E over k of genus 1 together with a point O 2 E.k/.

   (a)!(d). Let E be as in (a). Then E has genus 1 by formula (6), p. 32.
   (d)!(c). Let .E; O/ be as in (d). We shall see (p. 47) that the Riemann–Roch theorem
implies that the k-vector space L.3O/ has a basis 1; x; y such that the map

                             P 7! .x.P /W y.P /W 1/W E X fOg ! P2

defines an isomorphism from E onto the curve (9) sending O to .0W 1W 0/.
    (b)$(c). Let .E; O/ be as in (b). We shall show (Proposition 1.2) that a linear change
of variables will carry E into the form (c) and O into the point .0W 1W 0/. Conversely, let E
be as in (c); then O D .0W 1W 0/ 2 E.k/ and is a point of inflection (I, 1.20).
    (b)!(a). Obvious.
    On combining these statements, we see that a curve E as in (a) can be embedded in P2 in
such a way that O becomes a point of inflection. We also give a direct proof of this (p. 47).
    For definiteness, we adopt (d) as our definition.

Projective embedding of a curve of genus 1 with a rational point.
Let E be a complete nonsingular curve of genus 1 over a field k and let O 2 E.k/. According
to the Riemann–Roch theorem (I, 4.13), the rational functions on E having no poles except
at O and at worst a pole of order m  1 at O, form a vector space of dimension m over
k, i.e., L.mŒO/ has dimension m for m  1. The constant functions lie in L.ŒO/, and so
f1g is a basis for L.ŒO/. Choose x so that f1; xg is a basis for L.2ŒO/, and then y so that
f1; x; yg is a basis for L.3ŒO/. The set f1; x; y; x 2 g is linearly independent, hence a basis
for L.4ŒO/, because otherwise x 2 would be a linear combination of 1; x; y and could not
have a quadruple pole at O. Similarly, f1; x; y; x 2 ; xyg is a basis for L.5ŒO/.
     The subset f1; x; y; x 2 ; xy; x 3 ; y 2 g of L.6ŒO/ contains seven elements, and so it must
be linearly dependent, say,

                  a0 y 2 C a1 xy C a3 y D a00 x 3 C a2 x 2 C a4 x C a6 ;      ai 2 k;

as regular functions on E X fOg. Both a0 and a00 are nonzero because the set with either
x 3 or y 2 omitted is linearly independent, and so, after replacing y with a0 y=a00 and x with
a0 x=a00 and multiplying through by a002 =a03 , we can suppose that both equal 1. The map
P 7! .x.P /; y.P // sends E X fOg onto the affine plane curve

                     C W Y 2 C a1 X Y C a3 Y D X 3 C a2 X 2 C a4 X C a6 :

The function x has a double pole at O and no other pole, and so it has only two zeros.
Similarly, x C c has two zeros for every c 2 k (counting multiplicities), and so the composite
of the maps
                   E X fOg ! C ! A1 ; P 7! .x.P /; y.P // 7! x.P /;
has degree 2 (see I, 4.24, 4.26). Similarly, the composite of the maps

                     E X fOg ! C ! A1 ;         P 7! .x.P /; y.P // 7! y.P /

has degree 3. Therefore the degree of

                               P 7! .x.P /; y.P //W E X fOg ! C

divides both 2 and 3, and so equals 1, i.e., k.C / ' k.E/. If C were singular, then its
associated nonsingular curve E would have genus 0, contradicting our assumption. Therefore
C is nonsingular, and so the map is an isomorphism, and it extends to an isomorphism of E
onto
            CN W Y 2 Z C a1 X Y Z C a3 Y Z 2 D X 3 C a2 X 2 Z C a4 XZ 2 C a6 Z 3
(see I, 4.24c).

Assume that char.k/ ¤ 2. A nonsingular point P D .aW bW c/ on the curve CF is a point of inflection
if and only if H.a; b; c/ D 0, in which case I.P; CF \ H / D 1 if and only if the point of inflection

nonsingular cubic has 9 points of inflection in k al by Bezout’s theorem. Unfortunately, it might have
no point of inflection with coordinates in k. An invertible linear change of variables will not change
this (it will only multiply the Hessian by a nonzero constant). This explains why a nonlinear change
of variables was needed in the above argument.
A SIDE 1.4 An equation Y 2 D f .X /, deg f D 4, defines a singular curve whose associated nonsin-
gular curve has genus 1, and so is an elliptic curve if it has a rational point. Similarly, an intersection
of two quadric surfaces defines an elliptic curve if it has a rational point. See Cassels 1991, p. 35.

Isogeny is an equivalence relation. It is reflexive because the identity map is an isogeny, and
it is transitive because a composite of isogenies is an isogeny. Let 'W E ! E 0 be an isogeny.

Expansion near zero; the formal group of an elliptic curve
An elliptic curve, including its group structure, can be described “near” O by a single power
series in two variables. This is the formal group of the curve.

D EFINITION 2.6 A one-parameter commutative formal group over a commutative ring R
is a power series
                                  X
                      F .X; Y / D   ai;j X i Y j 2 RŒŒX; Y 
                                         i;j 0

satisfying the following conditions:
  (a) F .X; Y / D X C Y C terms of degree  2;
  (b) F .X; F .Y; Z// D F .F .X; Y /; Z/;
  (c) F .X; Y / D F .Y; X /.

These conditions imply thatPF .X; 0/ D X and F .0; Y / D Y and that there exists a unique
power series i.T / D T C n2 an T n 2 RŒŒT  such that F .T; i.T // D 0.
    Let F be a one-parameter formal group over a complete discrete valuation ring R with
maximal ideal m, for example, R D Zp and m D .p/. For all a; b 2 m, the power series
F .a; b/ converges to an element of m, and F defines a commutative group structure CF
on m.

3    Reduction of an elliptic curve modulo p
Consider an elliptic curve over Q. After a change of variables, the coefficients of its
Weierstrass equation will lie in Z, and so we may look at it modulo p to obtain a curve
                  def
over the field Fp D Z=pZ. The curve over Fp will still be a plane cubic curve, but it may
have a singular point. However, once the singular point is removed, the curve acquires a
canonical group structure. In this section, we first describe the algebraic curves having a
group structure defined by regular maps. Then we discuss singular cubic curves and their
canonical group structures. Finally, we describe the reduction of elliptic curves.
   Throughout, k is a perfect field.

Algebraic groups of dimension 1
Let C be a curve over k admitting a group structure defined by regular maps. Because k is
perfect, there exists a nonsingular point P in C.k al / (see 1.4d). It follows that every point
Q in C.k al / is nonsingular because the map R 7! R C .Q P / is a regular isomorphism
Ck al ! Ck al sending P to Q. Hence the curve is nonsingular.
     An algebraic group of dimension 1 over k is an algebraic curve over k equipped with
a group structure defined by regular maps. The following is a complete list of connected
algebraic groups of dimension 1 over k.
                        3. Reduction of an elliptic curve modulo p                             55


E LLIPTIC CURVES
These are the only projective curves admitting a group structure.

T HE ADDITIVE GROUP
The affine line A1 becomes a group under addition,

                         A1 .k/ D k;       .x; y/ 7! x C y W k  k ! k:

We write Ga for A1 endowed with this group structure.

We write Gm for A1 Xf0g endowed with this group structure. Note that the map x 7! .x; x 1 /
realizes Gm as an affine plane curve X Y D 1.

    We make a few remarks concerning the proofs of the above statement. We have seen
that if a nonsingular projective curve has genus 1, then it has a group structure, but why is
the converse true? The simplest explanation when k D C comes from topology. If M is a
compact oriented manifold and ˛W M ! M is a continuous map, then the Lefschetz fixed
point theorem says that

                                      L.a / D .     ˛ / D 0:

nent of M . On letting a tend to zero, we find that L.0 / D 0. But 0 is the identity map, and
so

We have shown that if M has a group structure, then its Euler–Poincaré characteristic is zero.
The Euler–Poincaré characteristic of a compact Riemann surface of genus g is 2 2g, and
so g D 1 if it has a group structure.
    A similar argument works over any field. If C is a projective curve with a group structure,

     To prove that Ga and Gm are the only affine curves C over k al admitting a group
structure, embed C in a nonsingular projective curve C 0 as the complement of a finite set S
of points. The translations by elements of C.k al / define an infinite set of automorphisms of
C 0 stabilizing the set S, from which one can deduce that C 0 D P1 and that S consists of one
or two points (see, for example, Milne 2017, 16.16). The extension of the statement to k is
an exercise in Galois cohomology (IV, 7.17).

Singular cubic curves
Let E be a singular cubic projective plane curve over k. Then E has exactly one singular
point S , and, because k is perfect, S has coordinates in k(see the argument p. 19). Assume
that E.k/ contains a point O ¤ S. Then the same definition as in the nonsingular case turns
         def
E ns .k/ D E.k/ X fSg into a group with O as its zero element. Namely, consider the line
through two nonsingular points P and Q. According to Bezout’s theorem and (I, 1.13), it
will intersect the curve in exactly one additional point PQ, which cannot be singular. Define
P C Q to be the third point of intersection with the cubic of the line through PQ and O.
This gives a group structure.
     We first examine this in two basic examples.

Reduction of an elliptic curve
Let E be an elliptic curve over Q, and let

              Y 2 Z C a1 X Y Z C a3 Y Z 2 D X 3 C a2 X 2 Z C a4 XZ 2 C a6 Z 3            (14)

be a Weierstrass equation for E. We make a change a variables
                               8
                                        2
                               < X 7! u X C r
                               ˆ
                               ˆ
                                  Y 7! u3 Y C su2 X C t                                  (15)
                               ˆ
                               ˆ
                               : Z 7! Z

with u; r; s; t 2 Q, u ¤ 0, chosen so that the new ai are integers and jj is minimal —
the equation is then said to be minimal. Two minimal equations are related by a change
of variables (15) with u; r; s; t 2 Z, u 2 Z . Most of the theory is independent of the
choice of the minimal equation. For example, the curve EN obtained by reducing a minimal
equation (14) for E modulo a prime p is well defined up to a change of variables (15) with
u; r; s; t 2 Fp , u ¤ 0; it is called the reduction of E modulo p
     When we are interested only in reductions modulo primes p ¤ 2; 3, we can work instead
with an equation
                              Y 2 Z D X 3 C aXZ 2 C bZ 3 ; a; b 2 Q:                  (16)
In this case, we make a change of variables X 7! X=c 2 , Y 7! Y =c 3 with c 2 Q chosen so
that the new a; b are integers and jj is minimal.
    An elliptic curve may have an equation of the form (14) that is “more minimal”, i.e., has
smaller discriminant, than any of the form (16). This does not matter for primes ¤ 2; 3, but,
for example,
                                     Y 2 C Y D X3 X2
defines a nonsingular curve over F2 whereas all equations of the form (16) define singular
curves (I, 1.5).

The different types of reduction
Let E be an elliptic curve over Q, given by a minimal equation (14), and let EN be its
reduction modulo p, given by the equation

              Y 2 Z C aN 1 X Y Z C aN 3 Y Z 2 D X 3 C aN 2 X 2 Z C aN 4 XZ 2 C aN 6 Z 3 ;

where aN i D ai modulo p.

G OOD REDUCTION
If p does not divide , then EN is an elliptic curve over Fp . For a point P D .xW yW z/ on
E, we can choose a representative .x; y; z/ for P with x; y; z 2 Z and having no common
                    def
factor, and then PN D .xN W yN W zN / is a well-defined point on E.
                                                                 N As .0W 1W 0/ reduces to .0W 1W 0/
                                                    N
and lines reduce to lines, the map E.Q/ ! E.Fp / is a homomorphism.

C USPIDAL , OR ADDITIVE , REDUCTION
This is the case where EN has a cusp, and so EN ns ' Ga . It occurs exactly when p divides
both  and c4 . For p ¤ 2; 3, it occurs exactly when p divides both 4a3 C 27b 2 and 2ab.

N ODAL , OR MULTIPLICATIVE , REDUCTION
This is the case where EN has a node. It occurs exactly when p divides  but not c4 . For
p ¤ 2; 3; it occurs exactly when p divides 4a3 C 27b 2 but not 2ab; then the tangents at
the node are rational over Fp if and only if 2ab becomes a square in Fp , in which case
EN ns ' Gm and E is said to have split multiplicative reduction. If 2ab is not a square
modulo p, then EN ns ' Gm Œ 2ab and E is said to have nonsplit multiplicative reduction.
                                                                                    N p /,
     In the nodal and cuspidal cases, there is a well-defined reduction map E.Q/ ! E.F
                                                                             N
which becomes a homomorphism when we omit the singular point from E.Fp / and any
points in E.Q/ mapping to it.

S EMISTABLE REDUCTION
If E has good or nodal reduction, then the minimal equation remains minimal after replacing
the ground field by a larger field. This is not so for cuspidal reduction. Consider, for example,
the curve
                               E W Y 2 Z D X 3 C pXZ 2 C pZ 3 :
After passing to an extension field in which p becomes a sixth power, say,  6 D p, we can
make a change of variables so that the equation becomes

                               E W Y 2 Z D X 3 C  2 XZ 2 C Z 3 :

Modulo  this becomes
                                        Y 2Z D X 3 C Z3;
which is nonsingular in characteristic ¤ 2; 3. In fact, for any curve E with cuspidal reduction
at p, there exists a finite extension of the ground field such that E has either good or nodal
reduction at the primes over p.
    In summary: good and nodal reduction are not changed by a field extension (in fact, the
minimal equation remains minimal) but cuspidal reduction always becomes good or nodal
                        3. Reduction of an elliptic curve modulo p                               61


reduction in an appropriate finite extension (and the minimal equation changes). For this
reason, a curve is said to have semistable reduction at p if it has good or nodal reduction.

Other fields
Throughout this section, we can replace Q and Z with Qp and Zp , or, in fact, with any finite
extension of Qp and its ring of integers. A minimal model for E over Q will remain minimal
over Qp . Also, we can replace Q and Z with a number field K and its ring of integers,
with the caution that, if the ring of integers in K is not a principal ideal domain, then it
may not be possible to find an equation for the elliptic curve that is minimal for all primes
simultaneously, i.e., that remains minimal over all the local fields.

R EMARK 4.3 Implicitly, we have assumed that p ¤ 2; 3. By working with the full Weier-
strass equation, it is possible to avoid that assumption.
     Similar statements hold for elliptic curves over any field K complete with respect to a
discrete valuation except that, when the residue field k is infinite, it is necessary to work
with a minimal equation in order for the quotient E.K/=E 0 .K/ to be finite, and the proof
of its finiteness is more difficult. Otherwise, the same arguments apply. In particular, if E
has good reduction and m is prime to the residue characteristic, then E.K/m ' E.k/  N m.

A SIDE 4.4 Instead of proving the above results directly, it is possible to deduce them from the
properties of the associated formal group.
    Consider an elliptic curve E over field K complete with respect to a discrete valuation, and
let R be the ring of integers in K, e.g., K D Qp and R D Zp . Choose a minimal Weierstrass
equation for E. In particular, the equation has coefficients a1 , a2 , . . . in R, which means that all the
power series in 2.7 have coefficients in R. Thus, we obtain a formal group law over R. The map
P 7! z.P / D x.P /=y.P / defines an isomorphism of groups .m; CF / ! E 1 .K/. See Tate 1974,
6.

A SIDE 4.5 Elliptic curves over Qp with split multiplicative reduction can all be described as quo-
tients Qp =q Z , i.e., as “Tate curves”. See Chapter III, p. 102.


5    Torsion points
For an elliptic curve E over Q (resp. Qp /, we fix an equation

                     Y 2 Z D X 3 C aXZ 2 C bZ 3 ;           D 4a3 C 27b 2 ¤ 0;

with coefficients a; b in Z (resp. Zp /. In particular, this allows us to speak of the coordinates
of a point on E. The torsion subgroup E.Q/tors of E.Q/ is the subgroup of points of finite
order.

T HEOREM 5.1 (L UTZ –NAGELL ) Let E be an elliptic curve over Q. If P D .xW yW 1/ 2
E.Q/ tors , then x; y 2 Z and either y D 0 or yj.

    The theorem follows from the Propositions 5.3 and 5.4 below, the first of which says that,
if P and 2P have integer coordinates, then either y D 0 or yj, and the second of which
implies that the torsion points all have integer coordinates.

R EMARK 5.2 (a) The theorem provides an algorithm for finding all the torsion points on E:
for each y D 0 or yj, find the integer roots x of

                                          X 3 C aX C b       y2

— they divide b y 2 — and then check whether .xW yW 1/ is a torsion point. This is faster if
the equation of E has been chosen to be minimal.
    (b) The converse of the theorem is not true: a point P 2 E.Q/ can satisfy the conditions
in the theorem without being a torsion point.
    (c) The theorem can often be used to prove that a point P 2 E.Q/ is of infinite order:
compute multiples nP of P until one has coordinates that are not integers, or better, just
compute the x-coordinates of 2P , 4P , 8P , using the duplication formula p. 52.

P ROPOSITION 5.3 Let E be an elliptic curve over Q and P D .x1 W y1 W 1/ a point of E.Q/.
If P and 2P have integer coordinates (when we set z D 1), then either y1 D 0 or y1 j.

 5.10 For an elliptic curve E over a number field K, the torsion points need not have
coordinates that are algebraic integers (when z is taken to be 1). Let Kv be the completion
of K at a prime lying over p, and let  be a prime element in Kv . The same argument as
above shows that there is an isomorphism
                               E n .Kv /=E 5n .Kv / !  n Ov = 5n Ov :
However, if p is a high power of  (i.e., the extension K=Q is highly ramified at v) and n is
small, this no longer excludes the possibility that E n .Kv / may contain an element of order
p.
 5.11 It was conjectured by Beppo Levi2 at the International Congress of Mathematicians in
1908, and proved by Mazur (1977, III, 5.1), that the torsion subgroup of E.Q/ is isomorphic
to one of the following groups:
                               Z=mZ              for    m D 1; 2; : : : ; 10; 12I
                            Z=2Z  Z=mZ          for    m D 2; 4; 6; 8:

By contrast, E.Qal /tors  Q=Z  Q=Z.

 5.12 The fact that E.Q/ tors is so much smaller than E.Qal /tors indicates that the image of

    An involution of a finite-dimensional Q-algebra R is a Q-linear map ˛ 7! ˛  W R ! R

T HEOREM 6.9 Let E be an elliptic curve over k, and let R D End0 .E/. Then .R;0 / is
isomorphic to one of the following:
  (a) Q with 0 the identity map;
  (b) an imaginary quadratic field F with 0 complex conjugation;
   (c) a quaternion4 division algebra Q over Q.
The third case occurs only in characteristic p ¤ 0, and Q is nonsplit at p and 1 and split at
all other primes.
P ROOF. We begin by listing some properties of .R;0 /, and then we show that any pair with
these properties is isomorphic to one on the above list.
  (i) R is a division algebra over Q (because, for every nonzero element ˛ of End.E/, there
      exists a ˇ 2 End.E/ such that ˛ˇ D m for some nonzero integer m; see 1.8).
 (ii) Every element of R satisfies a quadratic equation (see 6.2).
(iii) The map ˛ 7! ˛ 0 is a positive involution of R (see 6.8).
(iv) If char.k/ D 0, then R acts faithfully on a 2-dimensional vector space over Q (embed
     k in C, and let E.C/ D C=; then End0 .E/ acts faithfully on  ˝ Q; see III, 3.3).
 (v) For all primes ` ¤ char.k/, R ˝Q Q` acts faithfully on a 2-dimensional vector space
     over Q` (see 6.7).
   From (v) we see that ŒRW Q  4. Let F be the centre of R. Then
                                         ŒRW Q D ŒRW F ŒF W Q;
and it follows from a theorem of Wedderburn that ŒRW F  is a square. There are only four
possibilities, namely,
  (a) ŒRW Q D 1 and R D Q;
  (b) ŒRW Q D 2 and R a field (meaning a commutative field);
  (c) ŒRW Q D 4 and R a quaternion division algebra;
   (d) ŒRW Q D 3 or 4 and R a field.
In case (a), the only (positive) involution on Q is the identity map. In case (b), there is no
positive involution on R unless it is imaginary, in which case the only positive involution is
complex conjugation. Because of (iv), case (c) can only occur in nonzero characteristic p.
As R is a division algebra, it must be nonsplit at a nonzero even number of primes. From (v)
we see that it is split at all primes ` ¤ p; 1, and so it must be nonsplit at p and 1. Case (d)
is ruled out by (ii) and the primitive element theorem (FT, 5.1).                            2

    All possibilities in the theorem occur.
     4 A quaternion algebra R over a field k is a simple k-algebra of degree 4 with centre k. A field K  k

is said to split R if R ˝k K  M2 .K/. For a quaternion algebra R over Q, let S.R/ denote the set of prime
numbers or 1 such that Ql splits R. Then S.R/ is a finite set that determines R up to isomorphism. The sets of
the form S.R/ are exactly those with a finite even number of elements. The set S.R/ is empty if and only if
R  M2 .Q/. (See Wikipedia: quaternion algebra.)

Proof of Theorem 6.1
It remains to prove the first statement of Theorem 6.1. According to the elementary lemma
(IV, 4.8), for this it suffices to prove that deg satisfies the parallelogram law,

           deg.˛ C ˇ/ C deg.˛       ˇ/ D 2 deg.˛/ C 2 deg.ˇ/;            all ˛; ˇ 2 End.E/:

     For a geometric proof of the theorem, which applies to all abelian varieties, see Milne
1986a, 12.
     Alternatively, it is possible to prove directly that, for any endomorphism ˛ of E, there
exists a unique endomorphism ˛ 0 such that ˛ ı ˛ 0 D deg.˛/ D ˛ 0 ı ˛; moreover, .˛ C ˇ/0 D
˛ 0 C ˇ 0 for all ˛; ˇ (Silverman 2009, III, 6.2c). But now

              deg.˛ C ˇ/ C deg.˛           ˇ/ D .a C ˇ/.˛ C ˇ/0 C .˛         ˇ/.˛      ˇ/0
                                             D .˛ C ˇ/.˛ 0 C ˇ 0 / C .˛       ˇ/.˛ 0    ˇ0/
                                             D 2˛˛ 0 C 2ˇˇ 0
                                             D 2 deg.a/ C 2 deg.ˇ/

as required.
    Finally, in the rest of this section, following Cassels 1991, 24, we give an elementary
proof of parallelogram law.
    Let k D k0 .T /, and let P D .w0 W w1 W : : :/ 2 Pn .k/. We call .w0 W w1 W : : :/ a primitive

N OTES Most of the results in this section go back to Hasse in the 1930s. They play an essential
role in his proof of the Riemann hypothesis for elliptic curves (see IV, 9). Hasse (1936) proved that
the endomorphism algebra End0 .E/ of an elliptic curve E is Q, an imaginary quadratic field, or a
definite quaternion algebra. Deuring (1941) determined the structure of the endomorphism rings of
elliptic curves in nonzero characteristic.
     Over C, j. / is said to be a singular value of j if the corresponding elliptic curve E has
End.E/ ¤ Z. Thus, an elliptic curve E with End.E/ ¤ Z is an “elliptic curve with singular j -
invariant”. Eventually this was shortened to “singular elliptic curve”. By extension, Deuring called
an elliptic curve supersingular if its endomorphism ring has rank 4 over Z. In this case, he showed
that the endomorphism ring is a maximal order.
76                           Chapter II. Basic Theory of Elliptic Curves


7     Néron models
Recall that an elliptic curve E over Qp has a Weierstrass equation
         Y 2 Z C a1 XY Z C a3 Y Z 2 D X 3 C a2 X 2 Z C a4 XZ 2 C a6 Z 3 ;                    ai 2 Qp ;
which is uniquely determined up to a change of variables of the form
                                          X D u2 X 0 C r
                                          Y D u3 Y 0 C su2 X 0 C t
with u; r; s; t 2 Qp and u ¤ 0. Under such a change, the discriminant  transforms according
to  D u12 0 . We make a change of variables so that the ai 2 Zp and ordp ./ is as small
as possible. The new “minimal” equation is uniquely determined up to a change of variables
of the above form with u; r; s; t 2 Zp and u 2 Zp . We can think of this minimal Weierstrass
equation as defining a curve over Zp , which will be the best “model” of E over Zp among
plane projective curves. We call it the Weierstrass minimal model of E.
    Néron showed that if we allow our models to be curves over Zp that are not necessarily
embeddable in P2 , then we may obtain a model that is better in some respects than any plane
model. I shall attempt to explain what these Néron models are in this section. Unfortunately,
this is a difficult topic, which requires the theory of schemes for a satisfactory explanation5
and so the exposition will be very superficial. For a detailed account, see Silverman 1994,
Chap. IV.
    In order to be able to state Néron’s results, and the earlier results of Kodaira, we need to
expand our notion of a curve to allow “multiple components”. For an affine plane curve, this
means simply that we allow curves to be defined by polynomials f with repeated factors.
For example, the equation
                                   .Y     X /.Y     pX /.Y       p2X / D 0
defines an affine plane curve in the sense of Chapter I. It consists of three irreducible
components, namely, three lines through the origin. Modulo p, the equation becomes
                                              .Y     X /Y 2 D 0;
which is the union of two lines Y           X D 0 and Y D 0, the second of which has multiplicity
2.

                                                                                        1

                                                                         2




For more general curves, the idea is the same.
     5 Néron himself did not use schemes. For a long period, the only rigorous foundations for algebraic geometry

were provided by Weil 1946, 1962, which did not allow mixed characteristic. Consequently, those working in
mixed characteristic were forced to devise their own extension of Weil’s foundations, which makes their work
difficult to understand by the modern reader. This, of course, all changed in the early 1960s with Grothendieck’s
schemes, but some authors, Néron included, continued with the old way.

   Kodaira (1960) showed that, by blowing up points, and blowing down curves, etc., it is
possible to obtain from the surface

        S W Y 2 Z D X 3 C a.T /XZ 2 C b.T /Z 3 ;       a.T /; b.T / 2 CŒT ;   ŒT  ¤ 0

a new surface S 0 and a regular map S 0 ! A1 having the following properties:
  (a) S 0 is nonsingular;
  (b) S 0 regarded as a curve over C.T / is equal to S regarded as a curve over C.T / (for the
      experts, the maps S ! A1 and S 0 ! A1 have the same generic fibres);
  (c) the fibres E 0 .t0 / of S 0 over A1 .C/ are projective curves; moreover E 0 .t0 / D E.t0 /
      if the points of E.t0 / are nonsingular when regarded as points on S (for example, if
      E.t0 / itself is nonsingular);
  (d) S 0 is minimal with the above properties: if S 00 ! A1 is a second map with properties
        (a,b,c), then every regular map S 0 ! S 00 over A1 that is an isomorphism on the generic
        fibres is an isomorphism.
The map S 0 ! A1 is uniquely determined by these properties up to a unique isomorphism.
Kodaira classified the possible fibres of S 0 ! A1 .
    “Blowing up” a nonsingular point P in a surface S leaves the surface unchanged except
that it replaces the point P with the projective space of lines through the origin in the tangent
plane to S at P . For a curve C in S through P , the inverse image of C X P is a curve in the
blown up variety whose closure meets the projective space at the point corresponding to the
tangent line of C at P . Even when S  Pm , the blown-up surface does not have a natural
embedding into a projective space.

E XAMPLE 7.2 To illustrate the phenomenon of “blowing up”, consider the map

                               W k 2 ! k 2 ;    .x; y/ 7! .x; xy/:
                                             7. Néron models                                               79


Its image omits only the points on the Y -axis where Y ¤ 0. A point in the image is the
image of a unique point in k 2 except for .0; 0/, which has been “blown up” to the whole of
the Y -axis. In other words, the map is one-to-one, except that the Y -axis has been “blown
down” to a point. The line L W Y D ˛X has inverse image X Y D ˛X, which is the union of
the Y -axis and the line Y D ˛; the closure of the inverse image of L X f.0; 0/g is the line
Y D ˛. The singular curve
