4    Modular forms and the L-functions of elliptic curves
In this section, we first see that elliptic curves over Q are classified up to isogeny by their
L-functions, and then we see how the theory of modular forms leads to a list of candidates
for the L-functions of such curves.

Dirichlet series
A Dirichlet series is a series of the form
                                   X
                         f .s/ D       a.n/n s ;    a.n/ 2 C;         s 2 C:
                                 n1

The simplest example of such a series is, of course, the Riemann zeta function n1 n s . If
                                                                                    P

there exist positive constants A and b such that j nx a.n/j  Ax b for all large x, then the
                                                    P
series converges to an analytic function on the half-plane <.s/ > b.
                                                                                     a.n/n s and
                                                                                   P
    It is important to note that the function f .s/ determines  the a.n/, i.e., if
   b.n/n s are equal as functions of s on some half-plane, then a.n/ D b.n/ for all n. In
P
fact, by means of the Mellin transform  Pand itsninverse (see 4.3 below), f determines, and is
determined by, the function g.q/ D a.n/q , which is convergent on some disk about 0.
Therefore, the claim follows from the similar statement for power series.
    We shall be especially interested in Dirichlet series that can be expressed as Euler
products, that is, as
                                            Y         1
                                    f .s/ D                s/
                                                              ;
                                             p
                                                1   Pp .p
where each Pp is a polynomial and the product is over the prime numbers.
   Dirichlet series arise in two essentially different ways: from analysis and from arithmetic
geometry and number theory. One of the big problems in mathematics is to show that the
194                       Chapter V. Elliptic Curves and Modular Forms


second set of Dirichlet series is a subset of the first, and to identify the subset. This is a major
theme in the Langlands program, and the rest of the book will be concerned with explaining
how work of Wiles and others succeeds in identifying the L-functions of elliptic curves over
Q with L-functions arising from modular forms.

L-functions and isogeny classes
Recall (II, 1.8) that two elliptic curves .E; O/ and .E 0 ; O 0 / are said to be isogenous if
there exists a nonconstant regular map E ! E 0 sending O to O 0 , and that isogeny is an
equivalence relation.
    An isogeny E ! E 0 defines a homomorphism E.Q/ ! E 0 .Q/ which, in general, will be
neither injective nor surjective, but which does have a finite kernel and cokernel.6 Therefore,
the ranks of E.Q/ and E 0 .Q/ are the same, but their torsion subgroups will, in general, be
different. Surprisingly, isogenous curves over a finite field do have the same number of
points.
    6 For an isogeny ' of degree n, there exists an isogeny ' 0 such that ' ı ' 0 D n, and nW E.Q/ ! E.Q/ has

finite kernel and cokernel because E.Q/ is finitely generated.
                  4. Modular forms and the L-functions of elliptic curves                          195


T HEOREM 4.1 Let E and E 0 be elliptic curves over Q. If E and E 0 are isogenous, then
Np .E/ D Np .E 0 / for all good p. Conversely, if Np .E/ D Np .E 0 / for sufficiently many
good p, then E is isogenous to E 0 .

P ROOF. Let p be a good prime for E and E 0 . An isogeny ˛W E ! E 0 induces an isogeny
˛p W Ep ! Ep0 , which commutes with the Frobenius maps ' and ' 0 on E and E 0 . To see this,
suppose that

        ˛p .xW yW z/ D .P .x; y; z/W Q.x; y; z/W R.x; y; z//;            P; Q; R 2 Fp ŒX; Y; Z:

Then

            .˛p ı '/.xW yW z/ D .P .x p ; y p ; z p /W Q.x p ; y p ; z p /W R.x p ; y p ; z p //
            .' 0 ı ˛p /.xW yW z/ D .P .x; y; z/p W Q.x; y; z/p W R.x; y; z/p /

which the characteristic p binomial theorem shows to be equal. Now the diagram

                                                    1 '

commutes, and so
                            deg ˛  deg.1        '/ D deg.1        ' 0 /  deg ˛:
As

                                        deg.1       '/ D Np .E/
                                       deg.1        ' 0 / D Np .E 0 /

(see the proof of Theorem 9.4), this proves that Np .E/ D Np .E 0 /:
    The converse is much more difficult. It was conjectured by Tate about 1963, and proved
under some hypotheses by Serre. It was proved in general (for all abelian varieties) by
Faltings in his great article on Mordell’s conjecture (Faltings 1983).                   2

    The theorem of Faltings gives an effective procedure for deciding whether two elliptic
curves over Q are isogenous: there is a constant P such that if Np .E/ D Np .E 0 / for all
good p  P , then E and E 0 are isogenous. This has been made into an effective algorithm.
In practice, if your computer fails to find a p with Np .E/ ¤ Np .E 0 / in a few minutes you
can be very confident that the curves are isogenous.
    It is not obvious, but follows from the theory of Néron models, that isogenous elliptic
curves have the same type of reduction at every prime. Therefore, isogenous curves have
exactly the same L-functions and the same conductor. Because the L-functions is determined
by, and determines the Np , we have the following corollary.

C OROLLARY 4.2 Two elliptic curves E and E 0 are isogenous if and only if L.E; s/ D
L.E 0 ; s/.

     We therefore have a one-to-one correspondence:

In the remainder of this section we identify the L-functions corresponding to elliptic curves
over Q.
     As noted, isogenous curves over Q have the same set of bad primes S . A theorem of
Shafarevich says that, given a finite set of primes S of Q, there are only finitely many elliptic
curves over Q with good reduction outside S up to isomorphism. Hence, every isogeny class
of elliptic curves over Q contains only finitely many isomorphism classes.
     We sketch a proof of Shafarevich’s theorem. We may enlarge S and so suppose that
2; 3 2 S . Let ZS be the ring obtained from Z by inverting the primes in S . An elliptic curve

The function n 7! .n/ is called the Ramanujan  -function. In 1916, Ramanujan made
two remarkable conjectures,7 namely,
  (a) .n/  n11=2 .n/, where  .n/ is the number of positive divisors of n, and
      

    7 Sarnak (lecture 2018) states that “the two conjectures changed the theory of modular forms and of number

theory in the 20th century in a very major way”, and he calls the conjecture that .n/  n11=2 .n/ “one of the
most remarkable insights anyone ever had”.
                     4. Modular forms and the L-functions of elliptic curves              199


     For a prime p, (a) says that j .p/j  2p 11=2 . Readers will recognize that this will
be true if .p/ is the sum of two reciprocal roots of a polynomial “P11 .T /” occurring

hypothesis for algebraic varieties over finite fields was inspired by a theorem of Rankin that
j .n/j D O.n5:8 /. In turn, Deligne deduced Ramanujan’s conjecture from his theorem.
     Conjecture (b) was proved by Mordell in 1917 in a paper in which he introduced the first
examples of Hecke operators, which we now explain.

Linear algebra: the spectral theorem
Recall that a hermitian form on a vector space V is a mapping h ; iW V  V ! C such that
hv; wi D hw; vi and h ; i is linear in one variable and conjugate-linear in the other. Such a
form is said to be positive definite if hv; vi > 0 whenever v ¤ 0. A linear map ˛W V ! V is
self-adjoint (or hermitian) relative to h ; i if

                                     h˛v; wi D hv; ˛wi;          all v; w:

T HEOREM 4.16 (S PECTRAL T HEOREM ) Let V be a finite-dimensional complex vector
space with a positive definite hermitian form h ; i.
  (a) Every self-adjoint linear map ˛W V ! V is diagonalizable, i.e., V is a direct sum of
      eigenspaces for ˛.
  (b) Let ˛1 ; ˛2 ; : : : be a sequence of commuting self-adjoint linear maps V ! V ; then V
      has a basis of consisting of vectors that are eigenvectors for all ˛i .

P ROOF. (a) Because C is algebraically closed, ˛ has an eigenvector e1 . Let V1 be .Ce1 /? .
Then V1 is stable under ˛, and so contains an eigenvector e2 . Let V2 D .Ce1 ˚ Ce2 /? , and
so on.
                           L
    (b) We know that V D V .i /, where the i are the distinct eigenvalues of ˛1 . Because
˛2 commutes with ˛1 , it stabilizes each V .i /, and so each V .i / can be decomposed into a
directLsum of eigenspaces for ˛2 . Continuing in this fashion, we arrive at a decomposition
V D Vj such that each ˛i acts as a scalar on each Vj . Choose a basis for each space Vj ,
and take their union.                                                                       2


    This suggests that we should look for a hermitian form on S2k . 0 .N // for which the
T .n/ are self-adjoint.


The Petersson inner product
As Beltrami pointed out, the unit disk f.x; y/ 2 R2 j x 2 C y 2 < 1g forms a model for
hyperbolic geometry: if one defines a “line” to be a segment of a circle orthogonal to the
circumference of the disk, angles to be the usual angles, and distances in terms of cross-
ratios, one obtains a geometry that satisfies all the axioms for Euclidean geometry except
that, given a point P and a line `, there exist more than one line through P not meeting
                     z i
`. The map z 7! zCi      sends the upper half-plane f.x; y/ 2 R2 j y > 0g isomorphically
onto the unit disk, and, being fractional-linear, maps circles and lines to circles and lines
(collectively, not separately) and preserves angles. Therefore the upper half-plane is also
a model for hyperbolic geometry. The group of transformations of the upper half-plane
                                                    def
preserving distances and orientation is PSL2 .R/ D SL2 .R/=f˙I g, which therefore plays
the same role as the group of orientation preserving affine transformations
                                                                 ’ dxdy of the Euclidean
plane. The next proposition shows that the measure .U / D U y 2 is invariant under
                                                                                ’
transformations in PSL2 .R/, and therefore plays the same role as the measure U dxdy on
sets in the Euclidean plane.9

New forms: the theorem of Atkin and Lehner
The problem left in the last section has a simple remedy. If M jN , then 0 .M /  0 .N /,
and so S2k . 0 .M //  S2k . 0 .N //. Recall that N appears in the functional equation for
L.f; s/, and so it is not surprising that we run into trouble when we mix f of “level” N with
f that are really of level M jN , M < N .
     The way out of the problem is to say that a cusp form lying in some subspace S2k . 0 .M //,
                                                             old
M jN , M < N , is old. The old forms form a subspace S2k         . 0 .N // of S2k . 0 .N // whose
                              new
orthogonal complement S2k . 0 .N // is called the space of new forms. It is stable under
                                              new
all the operators T .n/ and wN , and so S2k       decomposes into a direct sum of orthogonal
subspaces Wi ,

                                     S2k   . 0 .N // D Wi ;
each of which is a simultaneous eigenspace for all T .n/ with gcd.n; N / D 1. Since the T .p/

R EMARK 4.21 Once the Hecke–Petersson theory had been developed, Ramanujan’s con-
jecture had an obvious generalization. Let f 2 S new
                                                 2k
                                                     . 0 .N // be a simultaneous eigenform
for the operators T .n/ with n prime to N , and write f D n1 c.n/q n with q D e 2 i s .
                                                             P
Normalize f so that c.1/ D 1. The Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture states that

                                            jap j  2  p .2k 1/=2 :

In other words, the roots of the polynomial

                                            T2     ap T C p 2k 1

have absolute value p .2k 1/=2 .
    Deligne (1971) showed that the roots of the above polynomial occur as the eigenvalues
of the Frobenius operator acting on the H 2k 1 of a nonsingular projective variety over Fp .10
Therefore, the Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture follows from the Riemann hypothesis for
the variety, proved in Deligne 1974.


5     Statement of the main theorems
Recall that to an elliptic curve E over Q, we have attached an L-function L.E; s/ D an n s
                                                                                       P
that (a) has coefficients an 2 Z, (b) can be expressed as an Euler product, and (c) conjecturally
satisfies a functional equation (involving the conductor N of E). Moreover, isogenous elliptic
curves have the same L-function (4.1). We therefore have a map
                                                              E 7!L.E;s/
                felliptic curves over Q up to isogenyg                   ! fDirichlet seriesg.

As noted earlier, Faltings (1983) shows that this map is injective: two elliptic curves are
isogenous if they have the same L-function.

P ROBLEM 5.1 Describe the collection of Dirichlet functions arising from elliptic curves
over Q.

   The theory of Hecke and Petersson, together with the theorem of Atkin and Lehner,
shows that the subspace S2new . 0 .N //  S2 . 0 .N // of new forms decomposes into a direct
sum
                                   S2new . 0 .N // D Wi
                                                    L

of one-dimensional subspaces Wi that are simultaneous eigenspaces for all the Hecke
operators T .n/ with gcd.n; N / D 1. Because they have dimension 1, each Wi is also an
    10 Under some restrictive hypotheses, but “the general case is not much more difficult,” according to Deligne

1974, p. 302.
208                       Chapter V. Elliptic Curves and Modular Forms


eigenspace for wN and for the T .p/ with pjN . An element of one of the subspaces Wi , i.e.,
a simultaneous eigenforms in S2new . 0 .N //, is traditionally called a newform, and we shall
adopt this terminology.
     In each Wi there is exactly one form fi D c.n/q n with c.1/ D 1 (said to be normal-
                                                 P
ized). Because fi is an eigenform for all the Hecke operators, it has an Euler product, and
because it is an eigenform for wN , it satisfies a functional equation (involving N ). If the
c.n/ are11 in Z, then L.fi ; s/ is a candidate for being the L-function of an elliptic curve
over Q.

T HEOREM 5.2 (E ICHLER –S HIMURA ) Let f D c.n/q n be a normalized newform for
                                                    P

  0 .N / of weight 2. If all c.n/ 2 Z, then there exists an elliptic curve Ef of conductor N
such that L.Ef ; s/ D L.f; s/.

    The early forms of the theorem (in the articles of Eichler and Shimura), were less precise
— in particular, they predate the work of Atkin and Lehner in which newforms were defined.
    The theorem of Eichler–Shimura has two parts: given f , construct the curve Ef (up to
isogeny); having constructed Ef , prove that L.Ef ; s/ D L.f; s/. We discuss the proofs of
the two parts in Sections 6 and 7 below.

D EFINITION 5.3 An elliptic curve E over Q is said to be modular of level N if its L-
function L.E; s/ is the L-function L.f; s/ of a normalized newform f of weight 2 for some
         12
  0 .N /.

    To complete the correspondence between elliptic curves over Q and normalized new-
forms of weight 2, we need to determine which elliptic curves are modular. Weil (1967)
surprised everyone by proving that they all are if their L-functions have sufficiently good
functional equations, i.e., satisfy a strong form of the Hasse–Weil conjecture.
    Recall that a Dirichlet character with modulus m is a homomorphism

                P
Dirichlet character  with modulus m prime to N , let
                          X                           m s
                L .s/ D     .n/an n s ;  .s/ D            .s/L .s/:

Weil proved, under some mild hypotheses on L.s/, that if the functions  .s/, where  runs

     Now consider an elliptic curve E over Q with conductor N . Recall that the Hasse–Weil
conjecture (10.3) asserts that the L-function of E satisfies a functional equation relating
.E; s/ and .E; 2 s/. Weil made the more precise conjecture “on certain theoretical
grounds” that this is also true for the modified L-functions  .s/. Together with his theorem,
this conjecture implies the following conjecture.

C ONJECTURE 5.4 Let E be an elliptic curve over Q with conductor N . Then the L-
function L.E; s/ equals L.f; s/ for some newform f of weight 2 for 0 .N /; in particular,
E is modular.

    Weil did not state 5.4 as a conjecture, but rather “recommended it to the interested
reader as an exercise”. Ogg, in his review of the article, referred to it as a “rather startling”
conjecture.
    For several years, Conjecture 5.4 was referred to as Weil’s conjecture. Then it was noted
that, in a set of problems circulated in Japanese at a 1955 conference,13 Taniyama had asked
in rather vague form,14 whether every elliptic curve over a number field is modular, and
so his name was added to the conjecture. It then transpired that Shimura, in conversation
with various mathematicians, had conjectured that all elliptic curves over Q are modular,
and so his name was also added. After a campaign by Serge Lang, some authors dropped
Weil’s name, and it became known as the Shimura–Taniyama conjecture. More recently,
mathematicians have referred to it as the modularity conjecture (now theorem).
    Whatever the history, it was Weil’s article that brought the conjecture to the attention
of the wider mathematical community, provided the first persuasive evidence for it, and
included the coincidence of the conductor of E with the level of f in the statement. The last
assertion made it possible to test the conjecture numerically: for a given N , list the (finitely
many) f for 0 .N /, list the (finitely many) isogeny classes of elliptic curves over Q with
conductor N , and check that the lists agree.
    In lectures in 1985, Frey suggested that the elliptic curve in II, 3.4, defined by a coun-
terexample to Fermat’s Last Theorem, has properties that are “so excellent that one suspects
that such a curve cannot exist”; in particular, it should not be modular (Frey 1986). This
encouraged Serre to rethink some old conjectures of his, and formulate two conjectures, one
of which implies that Frey’s curve is indeed not modular. In 1986, Ribet proved sufficient of
Serre’s conjectures to be able to show that Frey’s curve cannot be modular. We shall discuss
this work in Section 9.
    Thus, at this stage (1986) it was known that Conjecture 5.4 for elliptic curves over Q
implies Fermat’s Last Theorem, which inspired Wiles to attempt to prove the conjecture.
After a premature announcement in 1993, Wiles proved in 1994 (with the help of R. Taylor)
   13 The conference in question was the famous 1955 Tokyo-Nikko conference on algebraic number theory.

It was attended by most of the leading Western algebraic number theorists (Artin, Chevalley, Deuring, Néron,
Serre, Weil, . . . ) as well as the new generation of young Japanese mathematicians (Iwasawa, Kubota, Nakayama,
Satake, Shimura, Taniyama, . . . ).
    14 “Let C be an elliptic curve defined over an algebraic number field k, and L .s/ the L-function of C over

can be obtained?”
210                        Chapter V. Elliptic Curves and Modular Forms


that all semistable elliptic curves over Q, in particular, the Frey curve, are modular. Recall
that semistable just means that the curve does not have cuspidal reduction at any prime. In a
series of articles, Breuil, Conrad, Diamond, and Taylor improved the result so that it now
says that all elliptic curves E over Q is modular. In other words, the map
                                     f 7! Ef W ff g ! fE over Qg=
is surjective. This is the modularity theorem. We shall discuss the strategy of Wiles’s proof
in Section 8.

Construction of the elliptic curve over Q
We briefly explain why the above construction in fact gives an elliptic curve over Q. There
will be a few more details in the next section.
    For a compact Riemann surface X , we defined

This is a complex manifold, but as in the case of an elliptic curve, it is possible to construct
enough functions on it to embed it into projective space, and so realize it as a projective
algebraic variety.
    Now suppose that X is a nonsingular projective curve over an field k. Weil showed (as
part of the work mentioned on p. 159) that it is possible to attach to X a projective algebraic
variety Jac.X/ over k, which, in the case k D C becomes the variety defined in the last
paragraph. There is again a map X ! Jac.X /, well-defined up to translation by the choice
of a point P0 2 X.k/. The variety Jac.X / is an abelian variety, i.e., not only is it projective,
but it is equipped with a group structure given by regular maps.
    In particular, there is such a variety attached to the curve X0 .N / defined in Section
2. Moreover (see the next section), the Hecke operators T .n/ define endomorphisms
of Jac.X0 .N //. Because it has an abelian group structure, every integer m defines an
endomorphism of Jac.X0 .N //, and we define Ef to be the largest “quotient” of Jac.X0 .N //
on which T .n/ and c.n/ agree for all n relatively prime to N . One can prove that this
operation of “passing to the quotient” commutes with change of the ground field, and so in
this way we obtain an elliptic curve over Q that becomes equal over C to the curve defined
in the last subsection. On composing X0 .N / ! Jac.X0 .N // with Jac.X0 .N // ! Ef we
obtain a map X0 .N / ! Ef . More precisely, we have the following statement.

Brief review of the points of order p on elliptic curves
Let E be an elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field k. The map pW E ! E (multipli-
cation by p) is of degree p 2 . If k has characteristic zero, then the map is separable, which
implies that its kernel has order p 2 . If k has characteristic p, the map is never separable:
either it is purely inseparable (and so E has no points of order p) or its separable and
inseparable degrees are both p (and so E has p points of order dividing p). The first case
occurs for only finitely many values of j .

The Eichler–Shimura relation
The curve X0 .N / and the Hecke correspondence T .p/ are defined over Q. For almost all
primes p − N , X0 .N / will reduce to a nonsingular curve XQ 0 .N /.16 For such a good prime
p, the correspondence T .p/ defines a correspondence TQ .p/ on XQ 0 .N /.

A SIDE 7.11 The original papers on the Eichler–Shimura theorem are Eichler 1954 and Shimura
1958. In his paper, Eichler proved the Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture for some modular forms of
weight 2 by relating the conjecture to the Riemann hypothesis for curves over finite fields — he was
the first to make this connection. According to Sarnak (lecture 2018): “All of modern automorphic
forms in the connection with Frobenius, with algebraic geometry, with Grothendieck’s work, starts
with Eichler.”
     As noted in 4.21, the Ramanujan–Petersson conjecture has been proved by Deligne as a conse-
quence of his proof of the Riemann hypothesis for algebraic varieties over finite fields. However,
the conjecture has been generalized further to automorphic representations, and in that context there
remain many open questions. See Sarnak 2005.

Div0 .X / ! Jac.X /. The famous theorem of Abel–Jacobi says that this induces an isomorphism
J.X/ ! Jac.X / (Fulton 1995, 21d). The jacobian variety Jac.X / of a curve X over a field k
(constructed in general by Weil) has the property that Jac.X /.k/ D J.X /, at least when J.k/ ¤ ;.
For more on jacobian and abelian varieties over arbitrary fields, see Milne 1986a,b.

semistable elliptic curves over Q (i.e., curves with at worst nodal reduction).

    As we shall discuss in the next section, Serre in fact gave a recipe for defining the level

    By the early 1990s, there was much numerical evidence supporting Serre’s conjecture,
but few theorems. The most important of these was the following.

T HEOREM 8.6 (L ANGLANDS , T UNNELL ) If  W GS ! GL2 .F3 / is odd and irreducible,
then it is modular.

     Note that GL2 .F3 / has order 8  6 D 48. The action of PGL2 .F3 / on the projective plane
over F3 identifies it with S4 , and so GL2 .F3 / is a double cover SQ4 of S4 .
     The theorem of Langlands and Tunnell in fact concerned representations GS ! GL2 .C/.
In the nineteenth century, Klein classified the finite subgroups of GL2 .C/: their images
in PGL2 .C/ are cyclic, dihedral, A4 , S4 , or A5 . Langlands constructed candidates for the
modular forms, and verified they had the correct property in the A4 case. Tunnell extended
this to the S4 case, and, since GL2 .F3 / embeds into GL2 .C/, this verifies Serre’s conjecture
for F3 .
     Fix a representation 0 W GS ! GL2 .F` /. In future, R will always denote a complete local
Noetherian ring with residue field F` , for example, F` , Z` , or Z` ŒŒX . Two homomorphism
1 ; 2 W GS ! GL2 .R/ will be said to be strictly equivalent if

                      1 D M2 M          ;   M 2 Ker.GL2 .R/ ! GL2 .k//:

A deformation of 0 is a strict equivalence class of homomorphisms W GS ! GL2 .R/
whose composite with GL2 .R/ ! GL2 .Fp / is 0 .

     Now let E be an elliptic curve over Q, and assume initially that the representation of
GS on E3 is irreducible. By the theorem of Langlands and Tunnell, the representation

     What if the representation of GS on E.KS /3 is not irreducible, for example, if E.Q/
contains a point of order three? The representations of GS on E.KS /3 and E.KS /5 cannot
                                      9. Fermat, at last                                    223


both be reducible, because otherwise either E or a curve isogenous to E will have rational
points of order 3 and 5, hence a point of order 15, which is impossible by Mazur’s theorem
(II, 5.11). Unfortunately, there is no Langlands–Tunnell theorem for 5. Instead, Wiles uses
the following elegant argument.
     He shows that, given E, there exists a semistable elliptic curve E 0 over Q such that:
   (a) E 0 .KS /3 is irreducible;
  (b) E 0 .KS /5  E.KS /5 as GS -modules.
    Because of (a), the preceding argument applies to E 0 and shows it to be modular.
Hence the representation 5 W GS ! Aut.T5 E 0 / is modular, which implies that 0 W GS !
Aut.E 0 .KS /5 /  Aut.E.KS /5 / is modular. Now, Wiles can apply his original argument
with 3 replaced by 5.


9    Fermat, at last
Fix a prime number `, and let E be an elliptic curve over Q. As we now explain, it is possible
to decide whether or not E has good reduction at a prime p purely by considering the action
of G D Gal.Qal =Q/ on the modules E.Qal /`n for n  1.
     Let M be a finite abelian group, and let W G ! Aut.M / be a continuous homomorphism
(discrete topology on Aut.M /). The kernel H of  is an open subgroup of G, and therefore
its fixed field QalH is a finite extension of Q. We say that  is unramified at p if p is
unramified in QalH . With this terminology, we have the following criterion.
T HEOREM 9.1 The elliptic curve E has good reduction at a prime p ¤ ` if and only if the
representation of G on E.Qal /`n is unramified for all n.
    The necessity follows from Proposition 8.1. For the sufficiency, one can show from the
Néron model of E that, if E has bad reduction, then not all of its `n -torsion points can have
coordinates in Qun
                 `
                   .
    There is a similar criterion for p D `.
T HEOREM 9.2 Let ` be a prime. The elliptic curve E has good reduction at ` if and only if
the representation of G on E`n is flat for all n.
    For the experts, the representation of G on E.Qal /`n is flat if there is a finite flat group
scheme H over Z` such that H.Qal       `
                                         /  E.Qal
                                                ` `
                                                   / n as G-modules. Some authors say “finite”
or “crystalline” instead of flat.
    These criteria show that it is possible to detect whether E has bad reduction at p, and
hence whether p divides the conductor of E, from knowing how G acts on E.Qal /`n for
all n — it may not be possible to detect bad reduction simply by looking at E.Qal /` for
example.
    Recall that Serre conjectured that every P odd irreducible representation W G ! GL2 .F` /
is modular, i.e., that there exists an f D c.n/q n 2 S2k . 0 .N //, some k and N , such that

                                  Tr..Fp // D c.n/    mod `

whenever  is unramified at p.

is modular for a specific k and N . For example, a prime p ¤ ` divides N if and only if  is
ramified at p, and p divides N if and only if  is not flat.

    We omit the proof, which is difficult.

Such a cusp form would correspond to a holomorphic differential on X0 .2/, but X0 .N / has

proves that E does not exist.
A SIDE 9.5 Great problems are important because, like the Riemann hypothesis, they have important
applications, or, like Fermat’s Last Theorem, they reveal our ignorance and inspire great mathematics.
Fermat’s Last Theorem has certainly inspired great mathematics but it needs to be said that, even after
its solution, our ignorance of the rational solutions of polynomial equations over Q remains almost as
profound as before because the method applies only to Fermat’s equation (or very similar equations).
We do not even know, for example, whether it is possible for there to exist an algorithm for deciding
whether a polynomial equation with coefficients in Q has a solution with coordinates in Q.
N OTES Remarkably, Serre’s conjecture 9.3 has been proved. The level 1 case was proved by Khare
(2006), and the full conjecture by Khare and Wintenberger (2009). As described by Darmon 2017,
the proof uses a glorious extension of Wiles’s 3 – 5 switching technique in which essentially all the
primes are used. For an engaging account of the collaboration that led to the proof of the conjecture,
see Khare 2020.
N OTES Among the many works inspired by the proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem, I mention only
  ˘    the book Mozzochi 2000, which gives an engaging eyewitness account of the events surround-
       ing the proof,
  ˘    the book Diamond and Shurman 2005, which gives a much more detailed description of the
       modularity theorem and its background than was possuble in this chapter,
  ˘    the book of the 1995 instructional conference (Cornell et al. 1997) devoted to explaining the
       work of Ribet and others on Serre’s conjecture and of Wiles and others on the modularity
       conjecture, and
  ˘   the article Darmon 2017, which includes a brief summary of some of the work inspired by
      “Wiles’s marvelous proof”.


10     Epilogue
In a lecture in 2017, Serre roughly divided the history of number theory into two parts: the
abelian period from the time of Gauss and his quadratic reciprocity law through Artin and
his general reciprocity law to the mid 1960s, and the nonabelian period from the mid 1960s
to the present. According to Serre, the change was marked by Grothendieck’s introduction
of motives (about 1964) and Langlands’s announcement of his program (about 1966). We
briefly explain what these are, and their relation to elliptic curves.
                                       10. Epilogue                                      225


Motives
We have seen that the zeta function of an elliptic curve over Q decomposes as
                                             .s/  .s 1/
                                 .E; s/ D                 ;
                                                L.E; s/
where .s/ is the usual Riemann zeta function. The fundamental idea of motives is that
underlying this, and all similar decompositions, there is a decomposition of the “motive” of
E,
                              h.E/ D h0 .E/ ˚ h1 .E/ ˚ h2 .E/:
Here h1 .E/ has zeta function L.E; s/, and h0 .E/ has zeta function .s/, and so should
equal the motive h.P0 / of a point over Q. The zeta function of h2 .E/ is a shift of .s/,
which means that h2 .E/ should be a “Tate twist” of h.P0 /.
    Consider smooth projective varieties X and Y over a field k. An irreducible subvariety Z
in X  Y of codimension dim.X / defines a correspondence from Y to X — it can be thought
of as a many-valued map. The formal sums with Q-coefficients of such subvarieties, modulo
a suitable equivalence relation, form the Q-vector space C.Y; X / of correspondences from
Y to X . The smooth projective varieties over k form a category with the correspondences
as morphisms . The category of motives Mot.k/ over k is obtained from this category
by adjoining the kernels of idempotents and inverting the motive h2 .P1 /: Every smooth
projective variety X over k defines a motive h.X /, and motives over number fields define
zeta functions. For example, the motive h.E/ of an elliptic curve over Q does decompose as
described above. The conjecture of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer can be stated for an arbitrary
motive over a number field (Bloch, Kato, . . . ).
    It is possible to form tensor products of motives. When we take the equivalence relation
defining the correspondences to be numerical equivalence, Mot.k/ is an abelian category
whose Hom-groups are finite-dimensional vector spaces. When k has characteristic zero,
the subcategory generated by a finite collection of motives is equivalent to the category of
representations of a reductive algebraic group, called the motivic Galois group.
    For a brief elementary introduction to motives, see Milne 2013.

The Langlands program
What is the Langlands program? As Casselman says, it is all about L-functions.
     In number theory, many phenomena cannot be understood algebraically. For example, to
understand how the prime numbers are distributed, we need the Riemann zeta function. As
another example, Artin’s reciprocity law gives an algebraic description of the decomposition
of primes in an abelian extension of number fields, but there is no such algebraic description
for a nonabelian extension. Finally, we have seen that to understand the arithmetic of an
elliptic curve over Q, even its rational points, we need its L-function.
     In brief, since the time of Euler, number theorists have been introducing functions in
order to try to understand the phenomena they are studying. Usually, these are L-functions
that have Euler product expansions and (conjecturally) a functional equation of a very
specific type. The fundamental question is: what are the L-functions that arise in this way?
     In 1966 Langlands provided a conjectural answer. Given a reductive algebraic group
over Q, he described a way of constructing L-functions that have Euler product expansions,
and for each L-function, he constructed the various terms (N , the -factors, the constant
term, . . . ) that should go into its functional equation. These L-functions are said to be
226                  Chapter V. Elliptic Curves and Modular Forms


automorphic. When the reductive group is GL2 , they include the Mellin transforms of
modular forms. Every L-function arising naturally in number theory should be automorphic.
In particular, all zeta functions of motives should be automorphic. The modularity theorem
can now be interpreted as saying that the zeta function of the motive h1 .E/ of an elliptic
curve E over Q is automorphic.
